Executive summary [SOURCE: Page_3]

The International Joint Laboratory IJL “LECZ CARE” was born in the frame of the CARE laboratory (Centre Asiatique de Recherche sur l’Eau). CARE was created in November 2013 as one of the joint-laboratories of the RESCIF, the international network of French-speaking polytechnical schools. CARE has been built to create suitable conditions to gather Vietnamese and foreign scientists to address together key-issues on water and environment in the south of Vietnam. The CARE initiative has been -and is still- supported since the beginning by IRD, which provided several expatriations, long-term missions (MLDs) and equipment. After a five year “incubating” period (2014-2018), the Vietnamese and French scientists decided to apply to the IJL IRD call, considering that the CARE laboratory is a perfect hub for creating and conducting co-constructed researches at an international level. [SOURCE: Page_3]

The two main objectives of IJL LECZ CARE are a) to build research projects on the focused thematic of water resource and its evolution in the Low Elevation Coastal Zone (LECZ) in south of Vietnam, because the LECZ is threatened with severe environmental problems induced by human and climate change, and b) to create long term capacity building conditions with complementary thematic from HCMUT faculties and French labs to provide Master, PhD and post-doctoral co-supervisions of Vietnamese and French students. [SOURCE: Page_3]

The administrative organization of IJL is a classical one, implying regular meetings of a ‘Com-Dir’ for decision, a yearly “Scientific Day”, several seminar organizations, summer schools and a budget follow up totally transparent. The scientific organization relies on five “work-packages” as follows: [SOURCE: Page_3]

WP 1: Surface fluxes from land to ocean (D.T. Son/ J. Némery) WP 2: Fate and impact of organic contaminants (N. P. Dan / C. Baduel) WP 3: Hazard in coastal zones (Nguyen Thong/ H. T. Duc/ P. Marchesiello) WP 4: Vulnerabilities of natural water resources (Tu Tran Anh / M. Descloitres / S. Tweed) WP 5: Water Quality and Green Technologies (BX Thanh, K. Schonengerger, P. Rossi, N. P. Dan) [SOURCE: Page_3]

The overall activities of the work packages may be summarized with the IJL outputs, reminding that the COVID period slowed a lot some activities (field surveys and international exchanges). Also, the pre-IJL period was very fruitful and some outputs benefit a lot from the “incubation” period before 2018. [SOURCE: Page_3]

      Over the period 2014 - 2022, IJL LECZ-CARE researchers publish more than 75 ISI articles. CARE also participated to nearly 50 national and international scientific conferences. Since 2015, when the first publications came out of the CARE laboratory, among 39 publications listed in international journals on the subject of "Saigon river", 44% of them are from the Asian Water Research Centre. Among the 10 publications with the highest number of citations, 8 are from CARE. The most dynamic work package in term of number of publications is the work package n° 5 on waste water treatment, thanks to the close association between IJL LECZ CARE and the “Key Lab of Waste Water treatment”. [SOURCE: Page_3]

Statistics on publications for CARE lab (including IJL and JEAI publications). The close association with the VNU “Waste water treatment key-lab” boosts the publication dynamic. [SOURCE: Page_3]

 IJL involves a task force of 20 “etps” (“equivalent temps plein”-“equivalent full time”) with 14.55 “etps” for permanent staff (5.75 from French side, 8.8 from Vietnam). 30 permanent scientists are involved in IJL (17 from France, 13 from Vietnam) and 16 at more than 30% of their research time. Currently (2023), three PhDs and one post-doc are doing their research, helped by two technical staffs hired by the IJL. Since [SOURCE: Page_3]

2 [SOURCE: Page_3]

2019, the total amount of the task forces is between 15 and 20 “equivalent full time”, indicating a continuous involvement into the IJL. [SOURCE: Page_4]

     CARE and IJL LECZ-CARE scientists have initiated and conducted several actions for capacity building: a) student supervision (Vietnamese and French): 7 PhDs, 24 licenses or engineer internships (undergraduate) and 25 Master internships since 2014; b) Summer schools and c) Academic course organization. In total, since 2013, CARE has welcomed more than 100 mobilities (mostly student internships and around 20 “Missions de Longue Durée” IRD). Since 2019, due to the COVID pandemic, this amount has drastically decreased to less than 10. In 2023, more than 10 students from Europe are coming back to CARE. [SOURCE: Page_4]

During the past 10 years, IJL scientists and their students have gained significant expertise in coastal erosion and mangroves protection, in emerging contaminants (our expertise is now required by Saigon Water Supply Company-SAWACO), in groundwater resources vulnerabilities and recharge estimation (in a key-area for future needs), in setting long duration (10 years) of the Saigon River quality observatory (becoming a perfect base for long term environmental monitoring), in water treatment green technology, in the development of teaching modules for co-diplomation (PFIEV on “wastewater management”) and MOOC. In addition to a multipartite support form 3 French institutions (IRD, Grenoble INP, CNRS) and Vietnamese academic (VNU-HCM), the research projects funds are totalizing more than 600 k€ since 10 years. [SOURCE: Page_4]

The principal achievement of the IJL LECZ CARE Phase 1 could be finally to have built up the bases for future development of cutting-edge environmental sciences, complementary, with a better link with society needs, in an extremely sensitive developing region in the south of Vietnam, with all the threats that are now weighting on the human being. [SOURCE: Page_4]

INTRODUCTION As many scientific and partnership fruitful adventures are always the result of the combination of human initiatives and institutional supports, we would like to start this self-assessment report by giving some information about the history and the context of the creation of the International Joint Laboratory (IJL) LECZ-CARE. Indeed, IJL is a result of a strong partnership between Vietnamese and French (and westerners) scientists. It was born in the frame of the CARE laboratory (Centre Asiatique de Recherche sur l’Eau, http://carerescif.hcmut.edu.vn/en). CARE was created in November 2013 as one of the joint-laboratories of the RESCIF (www.rescif.net), the international network of French-speaking (fully or partly) polytechnical schools, with a starting impulse from Ho Chi Minh City University of Technology (HCMUT) as a member of the Vietnam National University-Ho Chi Minh City (VNU- HCM), Grenoble-INP, and a marked support of Ecole Polytechnique Fédérale de Lausanne (EPFL), Ecole Normale Supérieure de Lyon (ENS Lyon), Université Catholique de Louvain (UCL) and Poly-Montréal. According to the early vision of the first director of CARE, Mrs Phan Thi San Ha, CARE has been built “to create suitable conditions to gather Vietnamese and foreign scientists to address together key-issues on water and environment in the south of Vietnam ». This CARE initiative has been -and is still- supported since the beginning by IRD, that provided several expatriations, long-term missions (MLDs) and equipment. Besides, VNU-HCM has also provided annual supplementary support for administrative works, facility maintenance, seed research projects (the “YIPPs”, until 2019), sampling works and chemical lab. After a 5 year “incubating” period (2014-2018), and considering that the CARE laboratory is a perfect hub for creating and conducting co-constructed researches at an international level, [SOURCE: Page_4]

3 [SOURCE: Page_4]

the Institut des Géosciences de l’Environment (IGE, Grenoble), several faculties of HCMUT (Geology, Environment, Civil Engineering, Chemistry), jointly with several French laboratories (G-Eau, LEGOS, MIO) decided to apply to the IRD IJL project call in order to label the research activities on water-related issues on the ” Low Elevation Coastal Zones” for 2019-2023. The IJL creation has been politically supported by HCMUT, IRD, Grenoble- INP and lastly by CNRS, those four institutions being considered as the formal members of IJL. Consequently, IJL LECZ-CARE is considered as “the way IRD supports a reference centre project involving wider support from other French or international partners” (IRD “terms of reference”). Fig 1. CARE building as the result from a Grenoble INP and HCMUT initiative. The building is situated within HCMUT campus, and has five large offices for scientists, PhD students and director, a teaching room, a water analysis chemistry lab, a meeting room and a large student room, a rooftop terrace suitable for events and coffee breaks. [SOURCE: Page_5]

If numbering the “task force” at CARE, IJL was not the only IRD project for supporting CARE activities: indeed, the IRD-OCEAN department decided in 2018 to support the activity on plastic-related issues with the creation of the team JEAI “PLASTIC”1 for five years to enhance visibility of this very new scientific domain. This report focuses only on the IJL activities (i.e. without plastic-related researches), even if both IJL and JEAI were born from the same research hub (CARE). The success of the JEAI PLASTIC team and their willing to join the IJL for its second phase give us a strong motivation to enhance the links between water and plastics in the environment (see prospective project). At last but not least, in mid-2023, CARE laboratory has made a report and prospective exercise (very similar to the IJL renewal process) submitted to VNU and HCMUT in order to get a five to ten years supplementary funding. At the time of submitting the IRD IJL project (June 2023), the CARE performance Vietnamese report coupled with the prospective ten-year plan was accepted by HCMUT in this June 2023 and it is being submitted to VNU to apply for continuity of supplementary funding. We do expect to receive positive VNU’s answer in this September. No doubt that the IRD support (through a second phase of IJL) will be of definite advantage for supporting the Vietnamese CARE laboratory in order to bring CARE towards a fully operational structure for research and training for the next ten years. [SOURCE: Page_5]

### 1. Objectives of the IJL

The first motivation for creating the IJL was to comply with the initial ambition to build research projects together (i.e. Vietnamese and French – or westerners scientists) on the focused thematic of water resource and its evolution in the Low Elevation Coastal Zone (LECZ) in south of Vietnam, because the LECZ is threatened with severe environmental problems induced by human and climate change: subsidence of the Mekong Delta (MD), the Saigon-Dong Nai river basin and Ho Chi Minh City (HCMC), coastal erosion, saline intrusions along the coast and MD rivers, an increasing eutrophication in the Saigon river and canals, flooding inside the city, presence of pollutants and of Contaminants of Emerging Concern (CeCs) both in surface and groundwater. We are actually situated on a hot spot of the global environmental changes on the planet in a growing mega city (10 M inhabitants), in a region open to the world (as a “mega factory” for many industrial products) and close to the Mekong Delta considered as the “bowl of rice “of Southeast Asia. The second motivation was to address those challenges creating long term capacity building conditions with complementary thematic from HCMUT faculties and French labs to provide Master, PhD and post-doctoral co- supervisions of Vietnamese and French students. Actually, being hosted by CARE laboratory, IJL scientists benefit [SOURCE: Page_5]

1 Incorporating JEAI into LMI was foreseen by DISCO in 2018 but not possible due to several factors related to a) wishes of the JEAI [SOURCE: Page_5]

leader and correspondent - IRD representative in Vietnam, b) the “terms of references”' of both calls. 4 [SOURCE: Page_5]

from a common building, a water chemistry lab and an environmental observatory initiated in 2014 along the Saigon-Dong Nai river (and some coastal sites in MD). In addition, the academic frame of HCMUT polytechnical school is a perfect context to experiment innovative observations, new methods for environmental studies and technological solutions considering the crucial needs for water treatments and remediation (as green as possible). Therefore, the commitments of IJL LECZ-CARE scientists are to valorize the research actions on water-related issues, to create the necessary conditions to explore new ideas, gather initiatives around calls for projects, to strengthen international collaborations and enhance capacity building. If those objectives have been targeted, and somehow achieved, one should also note the Covid 19 pandemic consequences: for CARE and its IJL, the scientific activities and field campaign were reduced or even forbidden from March 2021 to April 2022 due to the severe lockdowns imposed by the authorities to limit the propagation of the virus (a full lockdown was even imposed for 12 weeks at home in the summer 2021). Exchanges outside Vietnam were also stopped for 2 years long from mid-2020 to mid-2022 due to the politic “zero-Covid” decided by Vietnam, which closed the boarders with the world. For an international partnership project, this situation was very troublesome. [SOURCE: Page_6]

### 2. Governance and partnerships

When the IJL project was built in 2018, the Vietnamese administrative director of CARE lab was also the IJL co-director (and IJL was named “Le CARE”). In mid-2019, HCMUT new president named another administrative director for CARE, and asked IJL” Le CARE” to change slightly its name to separate the IJL (considered at that time from the Vietnam side as a “project” rather than a “lab”) from CARE lab. We decided to rename IJL as “LECZ- CARE”. This led to the situation that the CARE administrative director was no longer the IJL co-director. From French side, the CARE scientific director is also the co-director of the IJL (Marc Descloitres, posted in Vietnam, replaced Nicolas Gratiot since mid-2020). However, as planned initially, the IJL organization remained melted into the CARE organization (Figure 2): [SOURCE: Page_6]

Figure 2. Schematic view of the CARE and IJL LECZ-CARE perimeters    The weekly executive “Com-Dir” meeting involves the CARE administrative director (Dr Ho Tuan Duc), the administrative deputy director (Mrs Nguyen Thi Huyen Trang), the IJL co-director (Dr Nguyen Phuoc Dan, formerly CARE director when IJL project was built), the JEAI leader (Dr Kieu Le Thuy Chung) and the CARE scientific director and IJL co-director (Dr Marc Descloitres). The notes of the meeting are shared between IJL members: https://docs.google.com/document/d/1zVXradboE8Zc^8xhnq^5mRxEviQJkXB4Yz^6mDgi^0lQt^4I/edit [SOURCE: Page_6]

5 [SOURCE: Page_6]

   The yearly scientific day and events are organized under the banner of CARE lab and involve IJL LECZ-CARE funding and results and JEAI “PLASTIC” project results. Each year we proposed to the deans of faculties and French UMR directors to participate annually to this scientific day in order to get their feedback. This interaction was not so efficient, and IJL could improve its organization to better receive external scientific and institutional advices.    The website of CARE, where IJL LECZ CARE and JEAI appear “only” as projects “hosted by CARE”. [SOURCE: Page_7]

After five years of functioning, this organization was found efficient to save time, combine efforts, share key- information, manage efficiently the financial grants. But, on the contrary, the visibility of IJL LECZ-CARE itself is not developed through a dedicated web site. This situation fitted however to the term of reference (i.e “IJL as the way IRD supports a reference center project involving wider support from other French or international partners”). During the current prospective period (may-June 2023) the name of IJL and its organization (governance) has been discussed: we would like to take the opportunity to come back to the original concept and to rename the IJL "LECZ-CARE" as IJL "CARE", Centre Asiatique de Recherche sur l’Eau (original name given by HCMUT and RESCIF). This choice has been made for three main reasons. Firstly, the JEAI PLASTIC has ended in 2022 and its research team will join the new IJL (becoming a new work-package: “microplastics”) meaning that all the research activities of the CARE structure will be now represented in the new IJL. Secondly, VNU allows the enlargement to other research teams outside the campus of HCMUT (as those teams are part of VNU) which perfectly fits to the new IJL scope to extend its partnership with other VNU universities. Last but not least, it avoids many confusions for Vietnamese partners as well as RESCIF partners who mainly know the structure CARE. As a consequence, the governance scheme will be simplified and is presented in the new prospective report. The overall partnership of IJL is too wide to be detailed here (for details, see the work packages projects). To summarize, locally, the partnership is based on the links between CARE and HCMUT faculties and a VNU key-Lab:    The Faculty GEOPET (Geology and Petroleum technology), for hydrogeology and geophysics,    The Faculty of Environmental and Natural Resources, for hydrology, hydrogeology, ecotoxicology    The Faculty of Civil Engineering, for oceanography, estuary hydrology.    The faculty of chemistry, for collaboration on waste water treatment      The VNU “key-lab” for Advanced Wastes Treatment (water and wastewater engineering, wastewater analysis and NbS). In addition, during 2022, several partnerships have been initiated in HCMC Universities: HCMUNRE, HCMUS. Regionally, several research projects have been built from 2014 with Can Tho University, with the Southern Institute of Water Resources Research, some IRD colleagues and partners in Cambodia and Laos. At last, internationally, in addition to the French UMRs (IGE, G-Eau, LEGOS and MIO), we initiated close collaborations with Grenoble INP laboratory, INRAE in Lyon, METIS UMR in Paris, INSA-DEEP in Lyon. Several projects allow some of IJL scientists to collaborate with Netherland, Korea, Japan, Switzerland, among others. [SOURCE: Page_7]

### 3. Institutional funding and financial functioning

Following the IRD IJL terms of reference, French and Vietnamese partners of LECZ CARE are able to provide some human, financial and material resources mobilized over the long term. Indeed, we received during the past five years a yearly “base functioning” grant from several sources that allow a global functioning yearly budget close to 55-60 k€. Figure 3 is showing the different sources taking the example of 2022.    From the Vietnamese side, CARE (including IJL) is granted by the Vietnam National University (VNU), who gives the yearly amount to the HCMUT University (close to 32.000 euros). In addition, HCMUT posts human resources (the CARE Director, three administrative staffs and one chemistry lab coordinator). Vietnamese scientists, who are working/joining with CARE, can find the potential funding for research, conference activities from local donors such as NAFOSTED (Ministry of Science and Technology), enterprises (SAWACO, VUCICO, Hitachi-Zosen, etc.) [SOURCE: Page_7]

6 [SOURCE: Page_7]

   From the French side LECZ-CARE “phase 1” was a “low cost” IJL (10.000 euros received yearly from IRD instead of 40.000 euros for classical projects). The other supports are from Grenoble-INP, CNRS (newly in 2020), IGE laboratory (and sometimes French embassy) but with variable amounts. [SOURCE: Page_8]

Fig.3. Example of funding of the IJL in 2022, jointly with Vietnamese financial support from VNU [SOURCE: Page_8]

After five years of functioning, we consider that even if this budget was found enough for the 2020- 2021 period (during lockdown and zero-covid period), for 2022 and 2023 this budget remains much too low for supporting new activities, insure the observations along the river and the corresponding costly analysis (eutrophication and Emerging Contaminants), develop “proof of concept” for new researches and supports the research on plastic and µ-plastic (the JEAI is completed since 2021) and, at last, to offer acceptable salaries for temporary staff (and avoid large turn-over). In 2022 and 2023, the laboratory IGE has understood the situation and granted temporarily the IJL with 20.000 euros as “seed money” for exploring new fields at CARE. The Figure 4 displays the global repartition of our yearly funds. The budget management is made using an excel shared file, revised each month by the two co-directors of IJL, with dialog with PIs of work packages. As an example, you may find the budget dashboard for 2020 to 2023 on the following share file: https://docs.google.com/spreadsheets/d/1ZrD-DEsTvz-iQjpwopMhgbR6WXVMsL6CpqXlY3iIv^6g/edit#gid=605835353 [SOURCE: Page_8]

Fig. 4. Example of the CARE and IJL 2022 budget (without research project funds) [SOURCE: Page_8]

IJL budget contributes to slightly more than the half of the CARE functioning budget. Note that VNU-HCM budget allow us to grant research paper through the attribution of “YIPP seed project”. However, this “seed project” grant became difficult to organize and, for future years, complementary funds should be found to fully operate “proof of concept” grants for new activities. The repartition of the expenses is very classical for a laboratory: the main expenses are international missions (10.000 euros), local surveys (6000 euros) for collecting water sample on the river (every 15 days for the Saigon 7 [SOURCE: Page_8]

river quality observatory), salaries for temporary staff (13000 euros), laboratory consumables and small equipment (15.000 euros). The rest are expenses for supporting local conference/workshops, gardening/cleaning. [SOURCE: Page_9]

### 4. Scientific organization

Considering the necessity to develop sustainable research combining multi-domain sciences, our vision was to build operational teams co-leaded by a Vietnamese and a French scientist^2, involving co-supervised Masters and PhD students. IJL has been built with five “work packages” focusing on societal issues that were the core issues of CARE at the beginning: i) Water and Critical Zone (CZ), ii) Water, hazards and vulnerabilities, iii) Water and health. The five workpackages are detailed in chapter 5.     WP 1: Surface fluxes from land to ocean in rivers (D.T. Son/ J. Némery)     WP 2: Fate and impact of organic contaminants (N. P. Dan / C. Baduel)     WP 3: Hazard in coastal zones (Nguyen Thong/ H. T. Duc/ P. Marchesiello)     WP 4: Vulnerabilities of natural water resources (T. A. Tu / M. Descloitres / S. Tweed)     WP 5: Water Quality and Green Technologies (B. X. Thanh, K. Schonengerger, P. Rossi, N. P. Dan) [SOURCE: Page_9]

### 5. Research activities and results

We choose to highlight some results for each work package, but of course, the scientific results are much more: the publication list of each WP illustrates this dynamic and interactions between WPs. [SOURCE: Page_9]

WP$_1$ : Surface flux from land to ocean in rivers Principal Investigators: Julien Némery and T. Son Dao [SOURCE: Page_9]

Summary of the objectives This WP examines the sources of nutrients (N, P) and carbon (C) and the biogeochemical transformations that take place in the salinity gradient of the Saigon River estuary. This WP aims to better understand the impact of the megacity of Ho Chi Minh City in terms of eutrophication, phytoplanktonic diversity and CNP flows towards the coastal zone. Is also focused on hydrological processes of the tidal system and urban water management. This WP is based on in situ monitoring, laboratory experiments and modelling. This WP is also in connection with WP$_2$ with the mutualization of the field campaign and the share of the database. [SOURCE: Page_9]

Main scientific results [SOURCE: Page_9]

Key-example 1: Implementation of C-GEM biogeochemical model >>> useful to test scenario of water quality recovery following urban planning. Nguyen TA et al (2021) Biogeochemical functioning of an urbanized tropical estuary: implementing the generic C-GEM (reactive transport) model. Science of The             Total            Environment           784,            147261 https://doi.org/10.1016/j.scitotenv.2021.147261 [SOURCE: Page_9]

Camenen B et al (2021) Monitoring discharge in a tidal river using water level observations: application on the Saigon River, Vietnam. Science of The Total Environment 761, 143195 https://doi.org/10.1016/j.scitotenv.2020.143195 [SOURCE: Page_10]

Key-example 4: Creation of an observatory of the quality of the Saigon river Since 2015, IJL LECZ CARE scientists and technical staff are surveying bi weekly the water quality of the Saigon River DongNai River system. Additional snapshot longitudinal profiles are surveyed in wet and dry seasons. This observatory produces valuable data on physico-chemical parameters (pH, T°C, conductivity, O$_2$), suspended sediment, nutrients (N, P, Si), organic carbon (dissolved and total) and chlorophyl a concentrations and phytoplankton biodiversity. Additional set of data on deposited sediment (C, N, P, trace metals) were also produced and already available in a datapaper (Nocent et al 2020 Data in Brief). This observatory benefits also for WP$_2$. [SOURCE: Page_10]

Publication dynamic WP$_1$ allowed to publish for the first time on eutrophication and nutrients cycling in the Saigon River. The paper Nguyen et al STOTEN 2019 is highly cited (15 average pub/year). Then the group of WP$_1$ had a dynamic publication activity around PhDs activities for a total of 10 articles in international journals. [SOURCE: Page_10]

Projects obtained WP$_1$ was supported by many projects that allowed to have a wide panel of approach from monitoring, lab experiment, modelling and projection in the future, focused on both River and estuarine systems and urban water management. For instance, NUTRIM project support the realization of the first longitudinal profiles along the river-estuarine system and the implementation of the C-GEM model. This support was clearly a booster for the WP$_1$ objectives and their realization. [SOURCE: Page_10]

WP$_2$ : Fate and Impact of emerging organic contaminants in surface waters PIs: Nguyen Phuoc Dan and Christine Baduel Objectives The main objective was to assess the impact of Ho Chi Minh Megacity (HCMC) on the water quality regarding emerging contaminants by integrating the river-estuary continuum from upstream HCMC down to the estuary mouth. We aimed to: (i) study the occurrence of CECs in the river-estuary continuum, (ii) evaluate the spatiotemporal dynamics of CECs and (iii) identify a list of prioritized substances for long term monitoring. Main scientific results [SOURCE: Page_12]

Key-example 1: Water samples were collected in 2019 and 2020 during the dry and the wet season along a 140-km stretch integrating the river- estuary continuum from upstream HCMC down to the estuary mouth in the East Sea. Additional water samples were collected at the mouth of the four main canals of the city center. Chemical analysis was performed targeting up to 217          micropollutants (pharmaceuticals, plasticizers, PFASs, flame retardants,        hormones, pesticides). Bioanalysis was also performed using six in- vitro bioassays for hormone receptor-mediated effects, xenobiotic        metabolism pathways and oxidative stress              response, respectively, all accompanied by cytotoxicity measurement. We are showing the results here of only one profile (mars 2020/dry season). A total of 120 micropollutants were detected and displayed high variability along the river continuum with total concentration ranging from 0.25 to 78 μg L−1. Among them, 59 micropollutants were ubiquitous to the system with a detection frequency ≥ 80 %. According to the land use higher concentration of pesticides were observed upstream the city center. The urban canals were identified as major sources of pharmaceuticals and industrial chemicals. By combining bioassays and chemical analysis, the contribution of the quantified and the unknown chemicals to the measured effects were assessed. Diuron, metolachlor, chlorpyrifos, daidzein, genistein, climbazole, mebendazole and telmisartan were then identified as main risk drivers among the chemicals quantified. Significance for community /stake holders Our work highlighted the importance of developing the management of anthropogenic pressures on water through agri-environmental and urban planning policy in order to ensure safe and sustainable drinking water production (drinking water catchment is located upstream of HCMC) and food production (aquaculture and mariculture sites are located along the estuary). Impact of a megacity on the water quality of a tropical estuary assessed by a combination of chemical analysis and in-vitro bioassays, Caracciolo R., Escher B.I., Lai F.Y., Nguyen T.A., Le Minh T.T., Schlichting R., Tröger R., Némery J., Wiberg K., Nguyen P.D., Baduel C.; Science of The Total Environment, Volume 877, 2023, 162525. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.scitotenv.2023.162525 [SOURCE: Page_12]

Concentration of CECs (ng.L-1)        Key-example 2: in the final drinking water          There is growing worry that drinking water can be products at different DWTP       affected by contaminants of emerging concern (CECs), including DWTP of HCMC.          potentially threatening human health. In this study, a wide range of CECs (n = 177), including pharmaceuticals, pesticides, perfluoroalkyl substances (PFASs) and other compounds, were analyzed in raw water and in drinking water collected from drinking water treatment plants (DWTPs) in Europe and Asia (n = 13). The DWTPs along the Saigon River and located upstream of HCMC was part of the study. We showed that among 177 targeted chemicals, 40 CECs were quantified in the raw water with a total concentration of 800 ng.L-1 while 20 CECs were still quantified in the drinking water final production with a total concentration of 400 ng.L-1. Pesticides were the more dominant CECs quantified in the raw and drinking water product. [SOURCE: Page_12]

Significance for community /stake holders [SOURCE: Page_13]

Drinking water contamination by CECs is an important topic as it my threat human health. Our work was very useful to SAWACO (Saigon Water Corporation), a public company dedicated to produce safe drinking water to HCMC’s population. Our work was presented at the SAWACO staff and they expressed their interest to set up a monitoring program for CECs. What’s in the water? – Target and suspect screening of contaminants of emerging concern in raw water and drinking water from Europe and Asia, Tröger R. et al.; Water Research, 198, 117099, 2021 https://doi.org/10.1016/j.scitotenv.2023.162525 [SOURCE: Page_13]

Publication dynamic WP$_2$ allowed to provide a farrow chemical diagnosis of emerging contaminants concentration and occurrence along the river-estuary continuum impacted by HCM Megacity. We identified priority chemicals that present a risk for ecosystems and for human health for long term monitoring. We also participated in an international study to assess the efficiency of DWTPs for the removal of CECs from surface water dedicated to drinking water production. WP$_2$ produced for now two papers of high relevance in high impact factor journals (STOTEN Impact factor: 10.7 and Water Research Impact factor: 13.4) [SOURCE: Page_13]

WP$_3$ – HAZARDS IN COASTAL ZONES PIs: Patrick Marchesiello, Thong Nguyen, Ho Tuan Duc [SOURCE: Page_13]

Objectives [SOURCE: Page_13]

The coastal Mekong delta has been generated by very large sedimentary fluxes eroded in mountain watersheds and redistributed by oceanic waves and currents. It is also now affected by human activity, including land subsidence, reduction of river fluxes due to damming and sand mining, and reduction of protective coastal mangroves in favor of agriculture and aquaculture. Our objectives emerged from a previous AFD/EU project (P. Marchesiello was the Team Leader) that revisited the paradigms needed for understanding and mediating coastal vulnerability: 1) assessing erosion rates and flood patterns; 2) assessing their hierarchy of causality 3) proposing engineering solutions. The main people involved at CARE were for the Vietnamese side (Civil Engineering, HCMUT): Thong Nguyen, Ho Tuan Duc, Tran Hai Yen and for the IRD side: Patrick Marchesiello and Nicolas Gratiot. [SOURCE: Page_13]

12 [SOURCE: Page_13]

Main scientific results [SOURCE: Page_14]

Key-example 1: Summary of processes                 During the AFD/EU LMDCZ project 2016-2018, we found at work in the coastal Mekong delta. that the Mekong Delta coastal zone is locally unbalanced. The observed erosion patterns can largely be explained by the natural redistribution of sediments by coastal currents. Away from the mouths, subsidence can explain coastal retreat in areas that should naturally advance (Gulf of Thailand). Our study of protection measures shows that that porous breakwaters can provide strong accretion (5-40 cm/yr) in the muddy coastal environment. This is good news and a change of paradigm compared with that used for sandy shorelines. Nevertheless, it should work only if the sediment supply is sufficient, which was not the case in Go Cong (river deficit due to dams). The outcome will therefore depend on the extent of policy development, which should also better incorporate land subsidence and the effect of dams based on their distance from estuaries. Marchesiello et al., Cont. Shelf Res. 2019. / Marchesiello and Dinh, AFD report 2017. [SOURCE: Page_14]

Key-example 2: The study of the relation between mangrove belt and coastal resiliency was introduced in the AFD/BRL expertise on Sea Defense Strategy (see projects obtained). Our results concurred with the literature that: 1) mangroves promote coastal accretion where sediment supply is adequate; 2) a large and healthy mangrove belt can protect a shoreline by enhancing wave dissipation, and 3) mangrove removal contributes to the aggravation of erosion. variation of Mekong delta However, we found that mangroves cannot accomplish their coastal shoreline change rates from 2003 to 2012 with mangrove width in           protection role under conditions of a failing sediment supply and 2012 in the three shoreline sectors.     under prevailing erosion. If these conditions are not factored in, the high expectations placed on mangroves to protect/stabilize the Mekong Delta coastline will be disappointed. Besset, M., Gratiot, N., Anthony, E. J., Bouchette, F., Goichot, M., & Marchesiello, P. (2019). Mangroves and shoreline erosion in the Mekong River delta, Viet Nam. Estuarine, Coastal and Shelf Science, 226, 106263 (download) [SOURCE: Page_14]

Other publications were more focused but of very good quality, such as those on the observation and modeling of coastal evolution in central Vietnam. [SOURCE: Page_14]

WP$_4$: Vulnerabilities of water resources (groundwater and surface water) Principal Investigators: Marc Descloitres, Sarah Tweed and Tu Tran Anh [SOURCE: Page_15]

Objectives: Improving our understanding of groundwater resources, vulnerabilities, river interactions in the Saigon river basin through:  Hydrogeophysical methods  the use of isotopes methods In addition, we implemented several capacity building actions in geophysics and hydrogeology, to gather team around the following questions:  Identify the main recharge zones and determine whether these zones are hotspots for contaminant transfers to the water table;  Identify how river-aquifer and aquifer-aquifer relationships as well as geological arrangement of aquifers affect water resource vulnerability. Main Scientific results [SOURCE: Page_16]

Key-example 1: Evaluation of the aquifer resource in the “refuge zone” of Cu Chi, North of Ho Chi Minh City This study is the subject of the PhD      of    Thanh Truong          Quoc, granted by ARTS thesis from IRD. The results are not published yet (Thanh is currently doing his 3rd year). In 2025, 1/3 of the Ho Chi Minh City will be partly or fully flooded due to subsidence and sea level rise. At 40 kms at the north of HCMC, there are more elevated areas (+15 m asl), that we could be consider as “refuge zones” because many activities will migrate towards the NW of the city. The objective of this research is to evaluate the groundwater resources, its vulnerabilities to pollution from the surface using geophysical techniques. A geophysical survey of 180 Time Domain Electromagnetism (TDEM) soundings in a typical area reveals discontinuous clayey 3D deposits inside the Pleistocene aquifer, arranged in paleo meander-shaped features. Geophysical data are not confirming the presence of clayey layer at the surface. Our results suggest that the aquifer arrangement derived from a transgression-regression cycles occurred in the past quaternary periods, and confirm the discontinuous nature of clayey deposits, rendering shallow aquifers vulnerable to infiltration of pollutants. This arrangement hasn’t been evidenced before and provides valuable information for future resources management. [SOURCE: Page_16]

Key-example 2: Localized recharge processes and implications for groundwater quality - Understanding recharge in the Mekong Delta is critical for the delta's groundwater resources, and requires the investigation of recharge processes at the local scale. In this study of the northeastern area of the Mekong         Delta,      time-series      of environmental tracer data (δ18O, δ2H, major ions and 3H) and markers of rural pollution (NH$_4$ and NO$_3$) were used to highlight localized recharge and impacts on groundwater quality. Results highlighted new hydrological insights into recharge processes, including that the Pleistocene aquifer receives recent recharge (< 60 years), predominantly during high rainfall months (> 100mm/month). However, due to shallow clay layers there are significant spatial variations in these recharge processes, which were observed in the seasonal fluctuation of groundwater δ18O values in groundwater. Wet season δ18O changes ranged from below analytical uncertainty (≤ 0.10‰) to up to 0.56‰, and the calculated fraction of rainfall contribution to the aquifer is ≤ 5 % to 16 %. Rainfall recharge via the acrisol soils results in low groundwater EC (20–55 μS/cm), acidic groundwater (pH 3.6–5.6), and may also have resulted in the low groundwater NO$_3$ concentrations (≤ 5.3 mg NO$_3$/L) at many sites due to adsorption, therefore delaying not reducing NO$_3$ contamination. Site specific variations in nitrogen processes includes increased NO$_3$ (to 29.7 mg/L) from fertiliser transfers or nitrification, and increased NH$_4$ (to 1.4 mg/L) likely due to the recharge of irrigation waters. These results should inform on priority areas for protection from further contamination by rural anthropogenic activities (Tu et al., 2022; https://doi.org/10.1016/j.scitotenv.2022.157118). [SOURCE: Page_16]

Publication dynamic The work package 4 is a new theme of research. It started in 2019 just before COVID pandemic: many field actions were delayed. The isotopes analysis was however performed thanks to boreholes drilled in 2019. Only one publication has been done, in 2022. We expect at least 2 more in 2023. [SOURCE: Page_16]

Main scientific results [SOURCE: Page_18]

Key-example 1: [SOURCE: Page_18]

Green roof (GR) for Sustainable Waste Water Management: [SOURCE: Page_18]

At a Hydraulic Loading Rate of 500 m^3/ha.day, the removal rate of COD, TN, and TP is 15 ± 7 gCOD/m^2.day, 1,23 ± 0,20 gN/m^2.day,and 0,031 ± 0,009 gP/m^2.day, respectively. [SOURCE: Page_18]

This new project has been built using IJL seed project funds in 2022 and 2023 (2x^3000 euros) Results are currently analysed [SOURCE: Page_18]

Key-example 2: [SOURCE: Page_18]

Application of membrane photobioreactor for wastewater treatment coupling with algae biomass cultivation for production of biomaterials: 3:1 is the optimal ratio for treatment performance sufficient removal of TN (86%), TP (79%) and COD (99%) and biomass productivity (1.12 g/L) [SOURCE: Page_18]

Nguyen, T. T., Binh, Q. A., Bui, X. T., Ngo, H. H., Vo, H. N. P., Lin, K. Y. A., Guo, W., Lin, C., Breider, F. (2020). Co-culture of microalgae-activated sludge for wastewater treatment and biomass production: Exploring their role under different inoculation ratios. Bioresource technology, 314, 123754. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.biortech.2020.123754 [SOURCE: Page_18]

Projects 2019-2023: [SOURCE: Page_18]

No       Project description 1        The project developed a green roof system, in which the local suitable plants are grown on the media of char coal and oyster shells. This system is located on the roof and it is used for municipal wastewater treatment and enhancing green area of the building/city. [SOURCE: Page_18]

2         The study focuses on application of a bioreactor system using coculture of microalgae and bacteria for both organic and nitrogen removals from the municipal wastewate without air supply 3         The research used a floating treatment wetland system including the specific plants floating on the water body (lake/canal/river) for nutrients and COD removals. 4a        The study presented a two-stage Anaerobic Membrane Bioreactor (2S-AnMBR) composed of a hydrolysis reactor (HR) followed by an anaerobic membrane bioreactor (AnMBR) for long- term co-digestion of food waste and kitchen wastewater. 4b        the long-term pilot study aimed to evaluate the performance of a 2S-AD plant for the treatment of municipal biowaste generated from HCMC. 5         The study aims to evaluate the efficiency of a pilot system, which contained a TF- ozonation unit, followed by BAC filtration, for long-term removal of DBP precursors and chlorine consuming substances from Saigon river water 6         The goal of this study was to evaluate the effect of DO, pH, alkalinity, nitrogen loading rate, and influent COD on the removal of nitrogen from an actual anaerobically pretreated husbandry wastewater in Vietnam using CANON-CSTR equipped with a simple aeration setup. 17 [SOURCE: Page_18]

7        Application of bamboo crop as a natural-based solution for sustainable urban management 8        The study used a submerged membrane filtration process coupled with powdered activated carbon for nonylphenol ethoxylates removal from the raw water for piped water supply in HCMC [SOURCE: Page_19]

Publication dynamic / quality of academic production and international influence In collaboration with CARE, Key Lab has published 21 papers, and 3 experimental systems since 2020. [SOURCE: Page_19]

### 6. Capacity building and special courses / summer schools

CARE and IJL LECZ-CARE scientists have initiated and conducted several actions for capacity building: a) Student supervision (Vietnamese and French), b) Summer schools and C) Academic course organization. Since 2013, CARE has welcomed more than 100 mobilities (mostly student internships and round 20 “Mission de Longue Durée” IRD)! Since 2019, due to the COVID pandemic, this amount has drastically decreased to less than 10. In 2023, more than 10 students from Europe are coming back to CARE. See mobilities on: https://docs.google.com/document/d/1dAtp^9An^9_NdSE_S12exzYtt^8JurXqpaCCq^2TfEgTRxA/edit# [SOURCE: Page_19]

6.1 Students supervision [SOURCE: Page_19]

Postdoc : only one postdoctoral position (granted by IRD) is currently under completion on the very new thematic of CeCs : Mrs Nguyen Tuyet had previously achieved a PhD in the frame of the WP$_1$, and her academic level allows a postdoctoral position at IGE. IJL and HCMUT will benefit from this capacity building in the near future. PhDs : during the last 5 years, IJL scientists have co-supervised 5 PhDs granted from several sources. Co supervision has been found as one of the best ways to co-construct research and develop shared science. In addition, three PhDs have been done inside WP 5 on waste water treatment, but without co-supervision.  WP 1 has co-supervised 3 PhDs covering the period 2018-2023 : Nguyen Tuyet's work on eutrophication of the Saigon River, Nguyen An's work on biogeochemical modeling of the Saigon River estuary and Francisco Amaral's work on satellite hydrological monitoring of the Saigon River. These thesis works were initiated and followed by the researchers involved in this WP (Julien Némery, Son Dao, Nicolas Gratiot and Christine Baduel).  WP 2 : 1 PhD student (2020-2023), Romane Caracciolo , works on Emerging Contaminants in the Saigon River at IGE and made her first field campaign in 2022 due to Covid restrictions. However, most of the analysis were undertaken tanks to a strong collaboration with CARE’s technical staff  WP 4: 1 1 PhD (2020-2023). This is the only “ARTS” PhD of IJL. Thanh Truong Quoc is a young lecturer of HCMUT and proposed for PhD by the GEOPET faculty. Currently, the work focuses on geophysical data. Masters students: 8 Vietnamese students (Master is a long duration diploma in Vietnam) have made their study at CARE. For example, A. Le Hoang. (2016-2019) worked on “Field and model investigation of flow and sediment transport in the Lower Mekong River”. Three French students and 1 from Morocco have worked on IJL subject art IGE, with 3 internships posted at CARE during several months. This amount is very low due to the COVID pandemic “zero-Coved” policy in Vietnam [SOURCE: Page_19]

Undergraduate students (engineer diploma): 21 Vietnamese students have work on IJL thematic (the majority with the association with the key lab and WP$_5$ on waste water treatment). Only 3 Master 1 French students have come to CARE for their diploma [SOURCE: Page_20]

6.2 Summer schools/workshops [SOURCE: Page_20]

Most of planned field school have been cancelled. In 2022, the only field school have been made in the frame of the PSF “HGP Mekong” project.  3 teaching sessions in Cambodia for hydrogeophysical methods (PSF “HGP Mekong” IRD project)  3 teaching sessions in Vietnam for hydrogeophysical methods (PSF “HGP Mekong” IRD project)  Training on Ion Chromatograph at CARE Lab  In France, Dr Yen participated into the GDRI (IRN) CROCO training in Barcelonnette (France) [SOURCE: Page_20]

6.3 Academic course organization [SOURCE: Page_20]

 PFIEV: Thanks to the initiative of Julien Nemery, Grenoble INP and HCMUT, a special cursus of engineer “PFIEV” (Programme francophone d’ingenieur d’excellence au Vietnam) on the topic “Urban Water management” has been created and partly based from IJL hydrological studies and observatory. A class started with ten students in 2022. A water quality/hydrology laboratory will be renovated at the Civil Engineering Faculty side. Dr Yen Tran is now the corresponding lecturer in charge of this PFEV module. This very new module is a very innovative module for Vietnam and this is thanks to CARE and IJL [SOURCE: Page_20]

 MOOCS: CARE laboratory has participated into the elaboration of the MOOC “Des rivières et des hommes » with RESCIF members. Several IJL scientist are still participating each year. We note the 8th session 2022; ~70 countries ; ~2,200 inscriptions ! https://www.grenoble-inp.fr/fr/l-institut/mooc-%C$_2$%AB-des- rivieres-et-des-hommes-%C$_2$%BB-la-saison-8-demarre-le-15- novembre-2020 [SOURCE: Page_20]

### 7. Conferences organization, expertise, dissemination activities

Most of the events at CARE are described on the CARE website: http://carerescif.hcmut.edu.vn/en/seminar- event [SOURCE: Page_20]

7.1 International conferences For the WP 1, as part as NUTRIM and NBS tools projects, some workshops were organized to share results and expertise. In 2018, the WP 3 organized a LMDCZ project workshop with AFD. CARE and IJL have sponsored and co-organized three important regional and international conferences held in HCMC in 2021 and in 2022 once the restrictions were over. We participated to organization committees. [SOURCE: Page_20]

   ICWRCE 2019 Danang, https://www.udn.vn/en_posts/view/619    GTSW        2019        :      https://www.sciencedirect.com/journal/environmental-technology-and- innovation/special-issue/10QQRSMJFMH    ICERES 2021 : https://conferences.hcmut.edu.vn/ICERES2021 [SOURCE: Page_20]

19 [SOURCE: Page_20]

WSGT 2022 : http://carerescif.hcmut.edu.vn/en/seminar-event/international-symposium-on-water- sustainability-and-green-technologies-wsgt-2022.html [SOURCE: Page_21]

7.2 Expertises In 2017 and 2018, CARE coordinated the Lower Mekong Delta Coastal Zone project, funded by the EU and AFD (1.2M€) (PI. Patrick Marchesiello, LEGOS). With scientific (Marchesiello et al., 2019; Besset et al., 2019; Ha et al., 2019) and operational (Dan and Marchesiello, 2018, final report) vocations, this project has resulted in a 20 M€ financing plan (EU/AFD-France) aimed at adapting the delta as best as possible to natural and anthropogenic changes [1]. In 2019, two expertises were developed: 1 : G. Vachaud and N. Gratiot, expertise for the Asian Bank for development (en sous traitance de la société SCE Nantes) : “Ho Chi Minh City Wastewater and Drainage Systeme Improvement”and 2: N. Gratiot was appointed as "mangrove-littoral erosion" expert for an AFD preparatory mission piloted by the engineering company BRL on behalf of AFD and in response to an Official Demand of Assistance from the Mekong coastal province. The final report was submitted to the Vietnamese authorities in the second half of 2020, following various meetings conducted by AFD, in conjunction with BRLingénierie. In 2023 Participation in the GEMMES expert report submitted by AFD to the Vietnamese ministerial level at the COP 27 side event (Gratiot, N., Anthony, E.J., Marchesiello, P. and Thong, N. 2022. Mangroves against coastal erosion?) AFD Focus report. The team participated also to “The Mekong delta emergency report 2022”, a final report of the GEMMES Vietnam project: “analyser les impacts socio-économiques du changement climatique et les stratégies d’adaptation”) [SOURCE: Page_21]

7.3 Dissemination activities After the COVID restrictions, a significant effort has been made to visit and present the IJL LECZ CARE and CARE laboratory to external close HCMC Universities. The objective was to reinitiated scientific collaborations after the severe restrictions with COVID. Most of the events are described in the CARE website page http://carerescif.hcmut.edu.vn/en/seminar-event [SOURCE: Page_21]

   At HCM University of Natural Resources and Environment HCMUNRE. Dr Duc, Dr Dan, M. Descloitres, F. Cazenave       and     S.     Hardy      have       made       several    presentations.    Example: http://carerescif.hcmut.edu.vn/en/seminar-event/Connaitre-la-taille-des-gouttes-de-pluie-pourrait- permettre-de-mieux-annoncer.html    At HCM University of Sciences (M. Descloitres, G. Uzu, Dr Dan) [SOURCE: Page_21]

20 [SOURCE: Page_21]

 At Institute of Environmental Research -IER (M. Descloitres, Dr Duc, Dr Dan)  At the Saigon Water Corporation (Dr Dan, Dr Du, Dr Tam, Dr Baduel) The output of those actions is the signature of three “MOU” with those institutions to enhance collaborations. Also, a promising research project has been initiated with the SAWACO. Towards the non-scientific world, several actions have been made, for example:  A « FLASH OSUG VIDEO ». Des algues dans nos cours d'eau : plus c'est vert mieux c'est ? (2021): https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=HkBlkN5yyck  Two conferences at the French Consulate with Dr Dan, and Dr Uzu: https://ho-chi-minh- ville.consulfrance.org/Table-ronde-La-pollution-de-l-air            and              https://ho-chi-minh- ville.consulfrance.org/Table-ronde-La-gestion-durable-de-l-eau  Several courses on environment and geophysics at the Lycée Marguerite Duras (M. Descloitres)  The         visit    of    the     French      Consulate    at    CARE        after    COVID      lockdown: http://carerescif.hcmut.edu.vn/en/seminar-event/visit-of-the-french-consul-at-care-laboratory-on- february-17th.html  Several papers in the periodic newspaper at Ho Chi Minh City “L’Echo des rizieres” https://www.afvsaigon.org//els/EDR/EDR129%20web.pdf [SOURCE: Page_22]

8. LMI's added value in terms of consolidating partnerships / Impact on the North- South scientific community and spinoffs in terms of development. [SOURCE: Page_22]

During the first ten years of CARE (and five years of LECZ CARE inside CARE), many missions, surveys have been initiated in Vietnam for French researchers and many scientific stays have been made by Vietnamese in French Laboratories. Thus, the consolidation of the partnerships has been “naturally” made building projects together, as the list of projects for each WP testifies. More than 70 publications have been co-authored by CARE and IJL scientists. Several high-level students have been co-supervised. Several projects and forthcoming projects have been made thanks to CARE. Since 2023, IJL members participate to three International Research Networks. [SOURCE: Page_22]

Conclusion [SOURCE: Page_22]

As conclusion to this self-assessment report, we would like to end with a list of actions, initiatives and projects that would probably not have been existing without CARE laboratory research hub and the financial support and organization from IJL LECZ-CARE, among them: [SOURCE: Page_22]

    The AFD Mekong expertise on the coastal erosion and mangroves     the SAWACO future project, which is in direct link with the society demand,     The development of teaching modules in modelling techniques, hydrogeophysics, creation of the PFIEV on “waste water management”, the MOOC that takes as example IJL river thematic,     The long duration (10 years) of the Saigon River quality monitoring, that is a perfect base for long term environmental monitoring,     The financial support from Grenoble INP and CNRS, who joint the IJL because we proposed a structure research organization,     The participation to two International Research Networks: the IRN Soot-Sea (initiated by IJL LOTUS in Hanoi) and the IRN CROCO for near shore ocean modelling solution, 21 [SOURCE: Page_22]

At last, but not the least, we acknowledge all the administrative and technical staff at CARE laboratory, who managed so many difficulties for administrative papers, among them the difficult task of getting visiting authorization for foreigners to stay in Vietnam and made possible some surveys during COVID time. As in many research projects, nothing could have been done without them! We include also of course the IGE administrative staff and the IRD administrative staff in Hanoi for decisive actions on the budget. Thanks to Trang, Phung, Huyen, Van, Tham, Ha Linh, Tam, Quang, Tin, Odette, Carole, Eric and their colleagues! [SOURCE: Page_23]

Annexes [SOURCE: Page_24]

1. Financial report The financial organization has been described in the new chapter 3 in this report. Below we give (remind) additional information on a) the general budget and b) the financial resources obtained by projects, calculated from the WP description. To provide the clearest possible view on the global finances, the com-Dir of CARE choose to share the budget dashboard for 2020 to 2023 on the following share file: https://docs.google.com/spreadsheets/d/1ZrD-DEsTvz-iQjpwopMhgbR6WXVMsL6CpqXlY3iIv^6g/edit#gid=605835353 Any scientist can, at any time, have a look to the budget. This file can be considered as a financial report of each year: each expense is identified with its the source of budget. As a reminder in this annex, the figure 4 displays the global repartition of our yearly funds. The budget management is made using an excel shared file, revised each month by the 2 co-directors of IJL, with dialog with PIs of work packages. [SOURCE: Page_24]

Fig. 4. Example of the CARE and IJL 2022 budget (without research project funds) [SOURCE: Page_24]

IJL budget contribute to slightly more than the half of the CARE functioning budget. Note that VNU Vietnamese budget allow us to grant research paper through the attribution of “YIPP seed project”. However, this “seed project” grant became difficult to organize and for future years, complementary funds should be found to fully operate “proof of concept” grants for new activities. The repartition of the expenses is very classical for a laboratory: the main expenses are international missions (10.000 euros), local surveys (6000 euros) for collecting water sample on the river every 15 days for the Saigon river quality observatory), payment of salary for temporary staff (13000 euros), laboratory consumables and small equipment (15.000 euros). Remaining expenses help for supporting local conference/workshops, gardening/cleaning. In addition to that general information, we would like to draw attention to the fact that one of the most important things when doing research project between foreign and Vietnamese scientists is the ability to spend the money easily and quickly along the year. To achieve this, the budget management gathers all the contributions of institutions and laboratories, and is used with the best flexibility possible. As a consequence, the implementation of IRD’s specific grant to the IJL cannot be explicitly separated from other expenses, and is then completely mixed with other donations. Note also that IGE lab is strongly supporting the IJL project as part of its main “activities at the south”, providing also “PRPT” vas a very important grant for hiring people. [SOURCE: Page_24]

Aside to the above described “base” budget, several research and capacity building projects have been obtained within the work packages, either from France, Europe or Vietnam. Those funding are “enough” for now to do the announced research, until 2023. Several research projects were funded by Vietnam like NAFOSTED and VNU-HCM, obtained by HCMUT's reserrcher who have collaborated with IJL. Some attempts (unsuccessful) to get large European funds have also been done (JPI Water / PI Christine Baduel). The projects are totalizing an amount of 645.000 euros between 2019 to 2023. They are detailed below, by work package. [SOURCE: Page_24]

### 2. Human ressources

2.1    Statistics Since the beginning, the people involved into the CARE and then IJL activities are noted on a shared document: https://docs.google.com/spreadsheets/d/14eAJsE4i^3oZ9fBdOjRT5HhhFhk^8mne^1woXoMiVMRooc/edit# gid=885685750 The overall “task force'' of IJL in 2023 is 20.55 “etps” (“equivalent temps plein”-“ equivalent full time”) with 14.55 “etps” for permanent staff (5.75 from french side, 8.8 from Vietnam). > 30 permanent scientists are involved in IJL (17 from France, 13 from Vietnam). If we retain people involved at more than 30% of their research time (excluding technical staff), 16 scientists (including PhDs and post-docs) are participating in the dynamic of IJL. > currently (2023), 3 PhDs and 1 post-docs are doing their research > 2 technical staffs are hired by the IJL (either full or half time) Since 2019, the total amount of the task forces remains between 15 to 20 “equivalent full time”, indicating a continuous involvement into the IJL. The WP$_5$ remains “officially” low, but the activities are often merged into the “Waste water Key-lab” and then IJL benefits from a very significant amount of people (as attested by the publication list) [SOURCE: Page_30]

Total WP$_1$ WP$_2$ WP$_3$ WP$_4$ WP$_5$ [SOURCE: Page_30]

3. IJL Outputs : 3.1 Publications list (rang « A », autres publications, ouvrages, chapitres d’ouvrages, communications scientifiques, communications vers les décideurs, communications vers le grand public, etc.), [SOURCE: Page_33]

Over the period 2014 - 2022, CARE has helped researchers publish around 75 ISI articles. Since 2015, when the first publications came out of the CARE laboratory, 39 publications have been listed in international journals on the subject of "Saigon river". 44% of them are from the Asian Water Research Centre and among the 10 publications with the highest number of citations, 8 are from CARE. CARE also participate to nearly 50 national and international scientific conferences. 65 papers are in the “Q$_1$” range. [SOURCE: Page_33]

30 25 number of papers) [SOURCE: Page_33]

20 15 10 5 Q$_1$ papers                           0 2015    2016    2017    2018    2019   2020   2021   2022       2023 Waste water, Water quality and 1       6        6         5       11       18              9         12         3 remediation Publications shared with VNU “waster water treatement key lab” Plastic and microplastic                                                      1             1      2         2          2 Hazards in water resources                                            1                     1 Hazards in coastal zones                                                      1       2     3      1         1 Human contamination (surface and 1        2       1       1     1 groundwater) Water quality and evolutions                                          2               3     3      2         2 [SOURCE: Page_33]

Statistics on publications for CARE lab (including IJL and JEAI publications). The close association with the VNU “Waste water treatment key-lab” boosts the publication dynamic. [SOURCE: Page_33]

The most dynamic work package in term of number of publications is the work package n° 5 on waste water treatment. This is due to the close association between CARE and the Key Lab of Waste Water treatment, created during the CARE incubating period (2014-2018). This also illustrate the fact that CARE can help in developing other structure of research and benefit from this dynamic also. [SOURCE: Page_33]

CARE Laboratory LIST OF PUBLICATIONS rule n°1: CARE, IJL LECZ CARE, JEAI PLASTIC, and their related funding institutions or partners must be acknowledged inside the papers using the following sentences, in addition to the affiliation to CARE from the authors who pertain to CARE.     for YIPP projects: “This research was conducted under the framework of CARE-RESCIF initiative and financially supported by Ho Chi Minh City University of Technology (HCMUT), VNU-HCM under grant number Tc-....”     for studies conducted with the support of CARE Laboratory (without YIPP support): “This research was conducted thanks to the (financial /technical/human) support of the CARE-RESCIF initiative (http://carerescif.hcmut.edu.vn/) within the (International Joint Laboratory LECZ-CARE / JEAI PLASTIC / other projects). In addition, we would like to thank Mr or Mrs or team XXXX for XXXX” [SOURCE: Page_34]

Rule n°2: What is a “CARE paper” ?: 1 ) paper with co-authors foreign-Vietnamese scientists who are collaborating in the framework of CARE sponsorship, 2) paper using data, facilities of CARE, 3) made by people working at CARE (i.e permanent people who are working physically inside CARE building), 4) people benefited from “CARE YIPP internal projects”, 5) papers which benefited from IRD, CNRS, Grenoble-INP, embassy research grants (PhDs, long stays, equipments), 6) paper under supervision of CARE permanent people, paper pertaining to the IJL work packages with direct relations to CARE facilities and/or financial support [SOURCE: Page_34]

in blue: IJL LECZ-CARE, all work packages, and previous CARE water-issues related papers (before IJL creation) in green: JEAI PLASTIC in black: other cases, from close collaborations. [SOURCE: Page_34]

Thematic priorities and scientific fields [SOURCE: Page_52]

Thematic priorities: Diagnose and explain environmental changes (e.g., climate, erosion, degradation of environment) with observations, laboratory or in situ experiments, modelling of predictive scenarios and investigation of remediation. [SOURCE: Page_52]

Scientific Fields: Hydrology; Coastal oceanography; Climate change; Biogeochemistry; Environmental chemistry; Drinking and Wastewater Treatment technologies; Membrane Technology; Nature based solutions; Risk assessment; Human geography; Human and social sciences; [SOURCE: Page_52]

51 [SOURCE: Page_52]

1. Executive Summary The Saigon-DongNai hydrological system and Mekong delta concentrate around 21 Million inhabitants (30% of Vietnam population) and 46% of Vietnam PIB (Đông Nai Statistical House, 2020). Ho Chi Minh City (HCMC) is the most populous city in Vietnam (10 million inhabitants), the financial capital and the prime economic hub. Due to intensive agriculture and aquaculture productions, the Mekong Delta referred to as the “rice bowl” is crucial on a socio-economic point of view for Vietnam. Because of their low elevation, the Mekong delta and the HCMC regions are threatened by climate variability and by human pressures at local, regional and global scales. In the region, climate change is manifest through sea level rise and through local changes in rainfall distribution and intensity. Relative sea level rise, due to both land subsidence and climate change, leads to increased coastal erosion and flooding, and increased saline water intrusion into the rivers and aquifers. As urbanization intensifies, natural environment becomes more and more degraded, making it increasingly challenging to ensure a healthy environment and a safe water supply for all. All these challenges involve water (its cycle, its quality, its social and economic importance). Water is the common denominator of the science developed in the International Joint Laboratory CARE, “Centre Asiatique de Recherche sur l’Eau”. Thus, the main objective of CARE is to strengthen research on all these regional challenges related to water and to intensify training and knowledge transfer in environmental sciences, hydrology, climate change modeling, water remediation and water treatment. To do so CARE will enlarge the scientific topics and expand its collaborative perimeter accordingly. The activities foreseen under CARE will strengthen the contribution of academic research to the adaptation to climate change and natural hazards, to the risk assessment related to pollutants emissions, to the identification of regulation and environmental infrastructure needs, and to the development of technologies or the implementation of nature based solutions capable to mitigate pollution for a healthy environment. Knowledge on water and climate are socio-environmental constructions that promote specific representations of nature. As this process frames and constrains environmental policies, CARE will include social sciences as a cross-cutting theme of four main thematic areas: 1) Water cycle: Understanding the dynamics of the Saigon-DongNai and Mekong river systems, groundwater budgets and severe floods induced by extreme rainfall events. 2) Anthropogenic pollution from their emissions sources, to rivers and down to coastal areas: Monitoring emerging pollution in environment (carbon and nutrient, microplastics, emerging contaminants, antibiotics, pesticides), characterizing their spatio-temporal dynamics, determining their fate and assessing their risk toward aquatic life and human health. Understanding Human practices with chemicals and human perception of environmental degradation. 3) Water Management: Developing engineering techniques that can provide solutions to provide drinking water and reducing the risks associated with water consumption; determining suitable nature based solutions to provide biodiversity benefits and to control water pollution from urban surface-runoff and municipal wastewater in urbanized areas. 4) Coastal Hazards: Coastal erosion and flooding, their relation to ocean variability, land subsidence, sand and sediment supply, as well as mean sea level rise, storm surges and tides. Creating a vulnerability map to flooding, erosion and sea-level rise for the south region and identifying how the population can adapt to these risks. CARE will be based on an excellent dynamic, integrating large newly funded projects (TROPECOS, SAWACO, PLUME, MULTISOURCE), and relying on trusted and productive partnership (see LECZ-CARE report). We will seize the opportunity to strengthen synergies between thematic areas that are still too compartmentalized and to develop transdisciplinary as a collaborative process that crosses disciplinary boundaries. All these research activities will of course involve post-doctoral researchers and students from Master to PhD who will be trained on various tools, from modeling to water quality monitoring and experimental laboratory works. A summer school on “Water quality and Emerging threats” will also be developed and opened to Master and PhD students from the South Vietnam region and Cambodian partners (we will apply to IRD DAM “PSF”). Through our research activities, we will establish a long term monitoring for water quality in collaboration with stakeholders (hydrodynamics, eutrophication, emerging contaminants, arsenic contamination in the Mekong Delta, air pollution). We will develop a socio-environmental database and upload it to the reliable and freely accessible database Datasuds. This observation system will be done by integrating all the data produced through the different research projects, our long-term monitoring and external data obtained from stakeholders (statistical offices, etc.). These research and training activities dedicated to HCMC and Mekong Delta region will serve to consolidate the role of CARE laboratory as a sub-regional center of excellence. A scientific hub where international and Vietnamese researchers can meet and work together. We will animate and support local research networks between HCMC and other universities in South Est Asia. During the next 5 years we will anticipate the ending of IJL program (if renewed) and secure the economic model of CARE laboratory for a bright future as described below. [SOURCE: Page_53]
