3. Research, training and transfer activities proposed: main objective, specific objectives, schedule, innovative scope of the project and expected results. [SOURCE: Page_55]

3.1 Main objective of IJL-2: [SOURCE: Page_55]

The renewal of IJL will be dedicated to two study areas that gather a large population and are key for the Vietnamese economy: the HCMC megacity and the Mekong Delta regions. These regions are threatened by local and global changes in human activities, in particular in terms of precipitation at high impact and flooding, rising relative sea level, polluted water linked to increasing urbanization and difficulties in accessing fresh and clean water. The overall objective of CARE is to strengthen research on those regional challenges, to investigate remediation strategies and to intensify training and knowledge transfer in hydrology, coastal oceanography, environmental sciences and human sciences. The CARE structure has been designed to foster exchanges between the scientific teams, but also to strengthen the communication and educational activities, the links with Vietnamese stakeholders and enhanced vietnamese north-south collaboration (with LOTUS IJL) as well as international collaborations. [SOURCE: Page_55]

Figure 1. CARE IJL Structure and connections [SOURCE: Page_55]

3.2 Specific research objectives and scientific activities: [SOURCE: Page_55]

The research objectives are to consolidate the teams and themes already implemented in LECZ-CARE and to continue to expand the research capacity for our close partners. The objectives of CARE will be also to develop new research topics on water challenges of socio-environmental importance such as: precipitation at high impact and risk of flooding, nature- based-solutions to mitigate risks and black carbon cycle across different environmental compartments (water, air). CARE will address the issue of microplastics in aquatic systems, by including the JEAI PLASTIC team, which has grown strongly over the last 5 years thanks to the support from IRD and from other funds. Moreover, as knowledge on water and climate are socio- environmental constructions, one key novelty of CARE is to include social sciences as a cross-cutting theme in order to include people at the heart of the science produced and to study the interrelationship between people and their changing and threatened environment. Another important objective of CARE will be to strengthen synergies between thematic fields that are still too compartmentalized and merge toward transdisciplinary as a collaborative process that crosses disciplinary boundaries. CARE will be organized around four main scientific topics, the “Core Themes’. The Core Themes and their related work packages are co-constructed and co-directed by Vietnamese/European researchers. The main objectives, the methodologies and the expected outcomes of each work package (WP) are presented below: [SOURCE: Page_55]

54 [SOURCE: Page_55]

Core Theme 1 (CT$_1$) : Water Cycle Quang Khai Ha and Cazenave F. [SOURCE: Page_56]

CT$_1$-WP$_1$: Hydrodynamics of tidal rivers [SOURCE: Page_56]

People involved: Camenen Benoit (INRAE), Ho Tuan Duc (HCMUT), Gratiot Nicolas (IRD/IGE), Pellarin Thierry (CNRS/IGE), Amaral Francisco (PhD CNRS), Le Coz Jérôme (INRAE) Context and knowledge gaps: Estuarine and deltaic areas are strongly influenced by freshwater flows (river flood), which are themselves modulated by tidal asymmetry. Understanding its hydrodynamics is crucial in terms of flood rise, saline intrusion or pollution. Scientific questions and main objective: One important issue for HCMC is to be able to evaluate the water discharge along the Saigon and Dong-Nai rivers in quasi real time. Thanks to continuous water measurements, it has been shown that water discharge can be evaluated locally with a correct accuracy (Camenen et al., 2021). Because of the strong spatial variability of the tidal effects, it is however necessary to provide a spatial view of the discharge. This could be achieved thanks to satellite data that could provide a snapshot of water levels and slope along the system. However, such methods need to be validated (PhD of F. Amaral). The Saigon tidal system is especially complex with possible interactions with groundwater, heavy rains, and canal networks of the city. One main objective is thus to be able to assess the main processes affecting the flow dynamics in such a complex tidal system depending on the hydro-meterological conditions. Material and Methods: The work package will use in-situ measurements (water level time-series, discharge gaugings) to calibrate models for evaluating water discharge. Coupled with SWOT satellite data, one could provide a spatial estimation of water discharge along the system for the specific time of satellite images. Eventually, these results will feed a 1D numerical model (Mage) that is currently built, including main HCMC canals (Master K. Gerarduzzi). This model, when calibrated and validated, will be an important tool to analyze the Saigon dynamics, as well as tracers (fine sediment, plastic, pollution, etc.). Expected outcomes and current project funding initiatives: Tools to evaluate water discharges from in-situ and satellite measurements; from 1D model to 2D and 3D of the Saigon-DongNai river system. Link with other work packages: There is a clear link with the two other work packages on the core theme CT$_1$: Water cycle (groundwater, precipitation) since both interact with the river dynamics. Also, this work package will feed the three work packages on anthropogenic pollution (CT$_2$) since any quantification of fluxes needs an estimation of the discharge dynamics. [SOURCE: Page_56]

CT$_1$-WP$_2$: Groundwater resources and recharge [SOURCE: Page_56]

People involved: Quang Khai Ha (HCMUT), Sarah Tweed (IRD/G-EAU), Lê Thị Minh Tâm (LECARE), Ngô Tấn Phong (HCMUT); Thanh Truong Quoc (HCMUT Geopet) Trần Thành Long (HCMUT), Marc Descloitres (IRD/IGE), Laurent Oxarango (UGA/IGE), Marc Leblanc (Univ. Avignon), Sylvain Massuel (IRD/G-EAU), Paul Baudron (IRD/G-Eau)/ Mouyyi Heng (ITC/Geology Fac.), Sambo Lun (ITC/ Geology faculty) Context and knowledge gaps: More than 55 million inhabitants of Vietnam are groundwater-dependent, and many households that use groundwater as their primary source of drinking water have private shallow bores (Carrard et al., 2019). Therefore, there remains an elevated health risk for inhabitants where shallow aquifers are exposed to contamination (including salinity, arsenic, or high NO$_3$ and high trace metal concentrations; Ha et al., 2022). In addition, the high groundwater pumping rates in the Mekong Delta have resulted in declining groundwater levels in all aquifers (Ha et al., 2019b; Duy et al., 2021). The over-exploitation has resulted in land subsidence and salinity intrusion along the coast of the Mekong River Delta (Stefan, 2014; Duong et al., 2010). Despite the heavy reliance on groundwater, there remain many questions relating to linked groundwater and contamination processes. In the Cambodian side of the MD, the use of surface water lead to several recharge processes during the dry season and one may suspect a significant pollution to groundwater with pesticides. The current test site of the JEAI 4C-WATER 4C will be studied with geophysical time lapse approach, a methodology that can be spread to other sites for projects, for studying salt water wedge evolution in coastal areas (in Cambodia -Sihanoukville and Vietnam- MD regions) Objectives: 1. Better constrain the interaction of groundwater with other compartments of the water cycle, including interactions with river systems, rainfall, and seawater. 2. Determine the implications for groundwater quality as a result of these interactions. 3. Assess the impacts of anthropogenic practices on groundwater interactions with other compartments of the water cycle and resultant contaminant transfers. Material and Methods: The seasonal variations in recharge processes, origins of water recharging the aquifer, and impacts of recharge on contaminants will be analyzed using environmental tracer data, including stable isotopes of water, major ions and tritium, as well as geophysical and modelling approaches. For hydrogeophysics, the IJL CARE will propose to pursue the current actions of capacity building of the IRD “PSF HGP-Mekong” by implementing course and training sessions on the new geophysical equipment of ITC Cambodia Institute and on “time-lapse” tomography. [SOURCE: Page_56]

55 [SOURCE: Page_56]

Expected outcomes: Gather information on the vulnerability of groundwater resources at both local and regional scales in the Mekong Delta. Presentation of the analysis of both hotspots and hot moments to protect groundwater in terms of both quantity and quality. New hydrogeophysical skills at ITC Cambodia for future projects. Links with other work packages the ‘Water Cycle’ core theme includes analyzing groundwater interactions with rivers (CT$_1$-WP$_1$) and impacts of rainfall recharge (CT$_1$-WP$_3$). In the core theme CT$_2$ on ‘Pollution’, links include the analysis of emerging contaminants (CT$_2$-WP$_5$) and microplastics (CT$_2$-WP$_6$). In the core theme (CT$_4$) on ‘Hazards in coastal zones’, the links are related to the analysis of subsidence (CT$_4$-WP12). [SOURCE: Page_57]

CT$_1$-WP$_3$: High Impact precipitation and flooding [SOURCE: Page_57]

People involved: Frédéric Cazenave (IRD/IGE), Can Thu Van (HCMUNRE), SRHMC, SIHYMECC Context and knowledge gaps: HCMC is one of the top ten cities in the world that is most likely to be severely affected by climate change, particularly floods, due to the intensification of the rainfall pattern on event and seasonal scales (Quan 2021, Phuong 2019, Vachaud 2019). However, none of the recent studies focused on time scales below the event level, which are crucial for understanding and anticipating intense rainfall leading to severe flooding. This is primarily due to the lack of appropriate instrumentation. As a result, the local operational services' weather and flood risk forecast information are not accurate enough to be considered as warnings that can initiate the protection of goods and people. Scientific questions and main objective: this WP will initiate actions to address two scientific questions related to rainfall in the Mekong Delta and HCMC regions: What are the spatiotemporal variability and dynamics of rainfall in the region? How can we identify and anticipate high-impact hydrometeorological events to support Vietnamese populations in a resilience process? The main objective of this WP is to characterize potentially high-impact precipitating events through fine-scale rainfall analysis to develop early detection tools. The WP aims to gather new Vietnamese hydro-meteorological partners around innovative tools for measuring intense rainfall in relation to floods through the following actions. Material and Methods: High-resolution and fast data acquisition are key factors in analyzing the spatiotemporal variability of heavy rain's influence on flooding. Since May 2022, a disdrometer has been installed on the CARE rooftop in Vietnam to study the climatology of rainfall in the Ho Chi Minh region for the first time. The Xport polarimetric weather radar (IGE/IRD), a powerful research tool for rainfall analysis, will provide real-time rainfall images at a very fine scale. Expected outcomes and current project funding initiatives: The expected outcomes are: (i) establish a small-scale precipitation database by collecting data from the disdrometer and radar, as well as data from local measurement networks, (ii) analysis of the climatology of high-impact precipitation, and (iii) enhance the Quantitative Rainfall Estimation by the local Nha Bé radar (SRMHC). The WP is partly funded by the Viet-Obs project (IGE funds). Other potential funding may be applied from the research programs of VNU-HCM, ANR, and MONRE. Links with other work packages: This WP is closely linked to other WPs in the Water Cycle core theme (CT$_1$) and all other WPs in which rainfall is a key factor, such as CT$_2$-WP$_4$ on Eutrophication and CT$_2$-WP$_7$ on Air quality. [SOURCE: Page_57]

Core Theme 2 (CT$_2$): Anthropogenic Pollution Kieu Le Thuy Chung and C. Baduel [SOURCE: Page_57]

CT$_2$-WP$_4$: Eutrophication and Greenhouse gases [SOURCE: Page_57]

People involved: Dao Thanh Son (FENR, HCMUT), Julien Némery (G-INP/IGE), Lê Thị Minh Tâm (CARE, HCMUT), Nguyen Phuoc Dan (FCE, HCMUT), Nguyen Van Tu (PhD ambassade/IRD), Nguyen Truong An (Post Doc TROPECOS) Context and knowledge gaps: During the first phase of IJL a hydrodynamic and biogeochemical model, the C-GEM model, has been implemented in the study area and now makes it possible to reproduce the spatio-temporal dynamics of the main explanatory variables of the eutrophication process (nutrient and carbon cycles, water balance, oxygen and phytoplankton). A first exploratory study also made it possible to estimate greenhouse gas emissions (CO2, CH4 and N2O) in relation to the level of eutrophication (Nguyen et al, 2022). Based on these findings it now appears necessary to assess the evolution of phytoplankton dynamics in relation to the contamination statut and hydrodynamic condition and to evaluate in more details the GHGs emissions. In addition, the biogeochemical model will be used to test future scenarios for the evolution of the impact of urban discharges. Scientific questions and main objective: This WP will focus on two main objectives: Evaluating the water quality based on phytoplankton diversity and environmental factors (hydrological and chemical) and estimating the GHGs emissions from this urban tropical estuary Material and Methods: The first objective will benefit from the existing database on environmental parameters and CECs, phytoplankton diversity. Additional samples will be collected according to the bi weekly monitoring support by CARE since 2015 and longitudinal profiles along the Saigon River and its estuary, especially during the PLUME campaign in 2024. The use of planktoscope, low cost biosampler of phytoplankton, will be tested to facilitate the acquisition of phytoplankton data. This work will be realize during the PhD work of Nguyen Van Tu (scholarship obtained for 2023-2028 from IRD/French Embassy). The second objective will be realized using C-GEM model and specific use of the model to quantify CO2 (and 56 [SOURCE: Page_57]

possibly CH$_4$) emissions. As part of TROPECOS project, the ambitious objective will be also to implement the model on two main branches of the Mekong Delta. This work will be realized by Nguyen Truong An (post-doc obtained for 2024-2026 within TROPECOS project). Expected outcomes and current project funding initiatives: Outcomes of this WP will to publish a datapaper of our monitoring program, to evaluate the link between CECs contaminations and phytoplankton biodiversity, and to estimate GHGs emissions from the Saigon River Estuary and the Mekong Delta Links with other work packages: The activities of this WP will be in close interaction with CT$_2$-WP$_5$ specially to evaluate the toxicity of CECs on phytoplankton but also with the core theme CT$_1$ for all aspect of hydrodynamics of the rivers. Finally, scenarii tested using C-GEM model will be in connection with outputs of urban sources control and pollution mapping with cross cutting theme on social sciences (XCT). [SOURCE: Page_58]

CT$_2$-WP$_5$: Contaminants of emerging concern [SOURCE: Page_58]

People involved: Lê Thị Minh Tâm (CARE, HCMUT), Baduel Christine (IRD/IGE), Nguyen Phuoc Dan (HCMUT), Nguyen Tuyet (Postdoc IRD/IGE), Cecile Miège (INRAE), Jean Martins (CNRS/IGE), Sophie Darfeuil (IRD/IGE), Céline Voiron (IRD/IGE), Armelle Crouzet (CNRS/IGE), To Thi Hien (HCMUS), Do Thi Thuy Quyen (HCMUS) Context and knowledge gaps: The expansion of HCMC inherently results in the spreading of a broad range of pollutants in the environment. Pollutants derive from anthropogenic sources, especially from untreated wastewaters, from agriculture, aquaculture and industry and from urban runoff and consequently end up in rivers, estuaries and coastal ecosystems. A wide range of chemicals has recently been identified as a potential threat to environmental and human health, the contaminants of emerging concern (CECs). In tropical environment and particularly in HCMC, very little is known about the CECs profile, their sources, their temporal variability (e.g. seasonal cycle, long-term trends) in the various aquatic environmental compartments (e.g. water, sediments) and their bioavailability and bioaccumulation along the food chain. Objectives: Our overarching objective is to study the behavior, fate and bioavailability of contaminants of emerging concern CECs (including antibiotics, antimicrobian resistant genes (ARGs), PFASs, pharmaceuticals, pesticides, plasticizers etc…) from the sources to the rivers and down to estuaries in Saigon-Dong Nai and Mekong Delta regions. Our specific aims are to: 1) Define the behavior and spatio-temporal dynamics of CECs in the dissolved and particulate phases. 2) Optimize low cost sampling strategies based on passive sampling and calibrate it for tropical and estuarine environments. 3) Establish the bioavailability of selected CECs and identify relevant biotransformation products in aquatic food web. 4) to estimate the kinetics of photo- and biodegradation of selected CECs in city canals and under irradiation experiments using Suntest chamber. 5) Estimate population exposure to emerging contaminants through drinking water and products from fisheries and provide a human risk assessment. Material and Methods: Grab sampling strategies together with integrated sampling strategies for suspended particulate matter (SPM) and surface waters will be deployed to assess time-integrated concentration of organic pollutants in aquatic system. Chemical analyses will be performed using state-of-this-art mass spectrometry techniques including target and non- target strategies (LC-MS/MS and LC-HR-MS). Instruments are available via collaboration with the CASE and HCMUS laboratories. Expected outcomes: Identifying core pollutants in aquatic systems and drinking water, establishing an environmental risk assessment to prioritize the CECs of interest for long term monitoring in surface water, establishing human exposure to CECs via drinking water and products from fisheries together with a human risk assessment. We will advise stakeholders for chemicals regulation and on the implementation of efficient environmental structures to mitigate the risk. Links with other work packages: Link with CT$_1$-WP$_1$ to estimate fluxes of CECs towards estuaries and CT$_1$-WP$_2$ to study the transfer of CECs toward groundwater resources. CT$_3$-WP$_8$ to assess the CECs that are not efficiently removed by DWTPs and to propose remediation strategies. Link with XCT for the identification of pollution sources and characterizing population practices (use of pesticides, antibiotics), perception of pollution. [SOURCE: Page_58]

CT$_2$-WP$_6$: Microplastics [SOURCE: Page_58]

People involved: Kieu-Le Thuy-Chung (GEOPET, HCMUT), Emilie Strady (IRD/MIO), Ngo Tan-Phong (GEOPET, HCMUT) Context and knowledge gaps: Due to the weaknesses of solid waste management, Vietnam was among the main contributors of plastic waste inputs to the oceans (Jambeck et al., 2015). In that context, the Party Central Committee XII prioritize the “Prevention, control, and significantly reduction of the pollution of the marine environment in order to become a regional leader in minimizing ocean plastic waste”. Since 2018, our research set up that microplastics, plastics being 0.001 to 5 mm in size, were found ubiquitous in surface water and sediment of various aquatic environments and biota in Vietnam (Lahens et al., 2018; Strady et al., 2020, 2021; Kieu-Le et al., 2022, 2023), being mainly released from wastewater treatment plants (Le et al, 2023). Still, comprehensive knowledge of microplastic pollution in the environment, fate and transport of microplastics needs to be fulfill. Scientific questions and main objective: This work package will focus on the following issues: 1. Spatial pattern of microplastics in specific aquatic environments. 2. Linkage between biofilms forming on microplastics, spread of antimicrobial resistance genes in biofilms and marine ecology. 3. The transport of microplastics in the porous media. 4. The microplastic fluxes released from point sources to the environment. 5. Automation of microplastic identification by machine learning. The 57 [SOURCE: Page_58]

main objective of the work package is to provide a holistic characterization of microplastic pollution in the environment in order to contribute to the stimulation of social action towards global plastic pollution issue. Material and Methods: Sampling and analysis of microplastic samples collected in specific aquatic environments. Incubating microplastics in marine environment and determining the antimicrobial resistance genes and bacteria present in microplastics. Sampling and analysis of microplastics released from potential point sources and determining the fluxes of microplastics released to the environment. Laboratory physical model and modeling the transport of microplastics in porous media. Expected outcomes and current project funding initiatives: Characterization of the spatial pattern of microplastic pollution in specific aquatic environment, the linkage of plastic pollution and antimicrobial resistance genes and potential threat to environmental and human health. The fluxes of microplastics release from point sources to the aquatic environment will provide knowledge for the policy makers in planning solutions of microplastic pollution. The knowledge of microplastics transport in porous media will help to understand the potential risk of microplastic pollution to groundwater. The fundings may be found from VNU-HCM, NAFOSTED, MarMiB project (GENØK - SENTER FOR BIOSIKKERHET, Norway), IRN PASSPORT- 2C. Links with other work packages: this work package will be liked with CT$_1$-WP$_1$ to study fluxes of microplastics and CT- WP$_2$ “Groundwater resources and recharges” [SOURCE: Page_59]

CT$_2$-WP$_7$: Emissions sources and Air quality [SOURCE: Page_59]

People involved: Tô Thị Hiền (HCMUS), Gaëlle Uzu (IRD/IGE), Jean –Luc Jaffrezo (CNRS/IGE), Pamela Dominutti (Postdoc IRD/IGE), Nguyen Ly Sy Phu (HCMUS), Do Thi Thuy Quyen (HCMUS), Nguyen Thi Thanh Nhon (assistant, HCMUS), Xavier Mari (IRD/MIO), Sophie Darfeuil (IRD/IGE), Céline Voiron (IRD/IGE) Context and knowledge gaps: Currently, knowledge is limited about the state of atmospheric contamination and sources of particulate pollutants in Vietnam, even in the largest cities. Few measurement campaigns have been conducted, and the few existing ones were short and focused on few pollutants. Like all countries in Southeast Asia, economic growth has a strong impact on air quality and human health, with multiple sources of emissions that are largely unregulated, including industries, burning of household, industrial, and agricultural waste, etc. Despite Southeast Asia being recognized as one of the most significant hotspots for mortality and morbidity associated with air pollution exposure, there is a notable absence of local cross-sectional studies examining air quality and health in the region. Scientific questions and main objective: The general objective is to propose a starting point for the study of air quality in Vietnam (aligned on IRN SOOT-SEA objectives), which will enable identifying the main sources of particulate pollutants in different urban and rural environments and their respective contributions to health exposure (through PM concentrations and health indicator so-called oxidative exposure) in particulate pollution. Another set of questions concerns the origin of these pollutants. Ultimately, the impact on the health of the population and environmental fate will be investigated through multidisciplinary studies in conjunction with epidemiological observations and ecotoxicological studies on Black Carbon. Material and Methods: A first step involves finalizing an ongoing demonstration study, including setting up a pioneering study site in HCMC and conducting a first series of annual samplings according to IRN SOOT-SEA practices. The partners will conduct joint chemical analysis of pollutants and train Vietnamese staff in France. The analysis will cover a broad range at the forefront of state-of-the-art particulate pollution characterization. The data will be processed as part of a joint thesis by an identified student. These developments will include setting up a more permanent infrastructure and samplings as part of this IJL and the IRN SOOT SEA, and studying gaseous pollutants and gas/particle transfers. Expected outcomes and current project funding initiatives: The expected outcomes include the identification of the main sources of particulate pollutants in the area of HCMC as well as the identification of the sources of sanitary interest. The HCMC site is already part of the IRN Soot-sea and PIs are thinking of raising local and international funds in 2024. Links with other work packages: This WP closely links with Emerging contaminants (CT$_2$-WP$_5$, WP$_6$)) and links with society (XCT). In fact, interactions can be done with analyzing emerging pollutants and microplastics in atmospheric samples, developing the link between pollutants and health, mapping pollution, studying precipitation pollution. [SOURCE: Page_59]

Core Theme 3 (CT 3): Water Management Phuoc-Dan NGUYEN and David RIASSETTO [SOURCE: Page_59]

CT$_3$-WP$_8$: Drinking Water Technologies [SOURCE: Page_59]

People involved: Riassetto David (LMGP), Phuoc-Dan Nguyen (HCMUT), Xuan-Thanh Bui (HCMUT), Huynh Ky Phuong Ha (HCMUT), Nguyen Cong Nguyen (DLU), Céline Ternon (LMGP), Damien Evrard (G-SCOP), and Chalida Klaysom (CU) Context and knowledge gaps: Water utilities in the Saigon Dong Nai and Mekong rivers basins face continuous challenges with the degradation of raw water quality. The rapid growth of urbanization, industrialization, and agricultural area expansion in the upstream areas have resulted in more and more increasing water pollution. Besides, all the current surface water treatment plants using the traditional water treatment processes do not remove efficiently hazardous emerging contaminants. In this context, the brackish water desalination is the most promising method for the community in the coastal 58 [SOURCE: Page_59]

areas, which currently face to saltwater intrusion caused by the climate changes or over-exploitation of freshwater from aquifers or surface water sources. Scientific questions and main objective: The scientific questions for the above challenges are: - What are the proper water treatment processes to remove refractory micropollutants, algal toxins and salts for the sake of safe drinking water supply for municipalities? How can we mitigate formation of Desinfection By Products (DBPs) in the chlorinated water for the current municipal water supply systems of cities in basins of Saigon-Dong Nai and Mekong rivers? What are the advanced water treatment methods, which may be employed for improvement of the current conventional treatment plants in Vietnam to achieve better treatment efficiency and operating and maintaining cost saving? The main objective of the WP is to identify feasible technical measures for improving the conventional water treatment plants to remove the contaminants of emerging concerns, DOC, agal toxins and mitigate DBPs. Material and Methods: Methodology for this WP is illustrated by the following works: The presence of refractory contaminants/toxins in the raw water resources used for municipal water supply systems will be assessed quantitatively. The advanced water treatment processes such as activated carbon adsorption, membrane treatment, ozone, ultraviolet (UV) disinfection, and biofiltration, or a combination thereof may be used. Laboratory or pilot-scale experiments for the advanced processes will be set-up and operated to find the suitable design parameters and operating conditions. Expected outcomes and current project funding initiatives: The expected outcomes are: (i) an assessment of the main contaminants, which may cause hazards for community health through the portable water consumption, and (ii) the identification of feasible water treatment methods for the conventional WTPs to remove further the emerging contaminants. The SAWACO project, which aims to assess quantitatively the presence of CECs and DBPs in the water supply system managed by Saigon Water Supply COmpany, is financially supported by SAWACO and DOST HCMC. The other potential fundings may be applied from the research programs of VNU-HCM, INGOs, DOST of provinces/cities and NAFOSTED. Links with other work packages: This WP closely links with CT$_2$-WP$_5$ on ‘Emerging contaminants’ and CT$_2$-WP$_4$ on ‘Eutrophication’. [SOURCE: Page_60]

CT$_3$-WP$_9$: Nature Based Solutions (NBS) [SOURCE: Page_60]

People involved: Xuan-Thanh Bui (HCMUT), Jaime Nivala (INRAE), Julien Némery (Grenoble INP), Riassetto David (LMGP), Phuoc-Dan Nguyen (HCMUT), Ngo Thi Diem Trang (CTU), Vo Thi Kim Uyen (HCMUT), Castebrunet Hélène (UGA/IGE) Context and knowledge gaps: Globally, sea level rising, coastal erosion, extended dry periods, and floodings contribute to decreased water security and increased disaster incidence in the populous cities in Vietnam. Nature-Based Solutions (NBS) are increasingly being promoted as innovative measures to mitigate environmental stresses in urban areas such as air and water pollution as well as coupling with increase of green space simultaneously provide human well-being and biodiversity benefits (European Commission, 2015; IUCN, 2020). However, the widespread application of NBS still has been limited, due in part to the perceived high costs associated with their operation and maintenance. Scientific questions and main objective: The scientific questions on application of NBSs are: What are the applicable NBSs for water pollution controls coupled with increasing green space in cities in Vietnam? How can we can convince the community and government to implement NBS into the practice in Vietnam? The main objective of the WP is to determine the suitable NBSs for enhancement of urban green space to provide biodiversity benefits and to control water pollution from urban surface-runoff and municipal wastewater in cities in Vietnam. Material and Methods: The methodology of the WP will be developed as follows: To deliver an overview on potential benefits provided by NBS; To set-up and operating the lab-scale and pilot-scale experiments of the NBSs To analyze performance of NBSs in terms of environmental and social benefits, and investment and O&M costs. Life cycle assessment of NBS applications will be conducted. Expected outcomes and current project funding initiatives: The expected outcomes of the WP are: (i) to determine potential applications of NBSs for sustainable urban water management in the big cities in Vietnam and (ii) to determine quantitatively environmental and cost benefits of NBS applications. The current MULTISOURCEs project, which focuses on NBS applications, has received funding from the European Union’s Horizon H2020 innovation action program. The other potential fundings may be found from research programs of VNU-HCM, IRD, NGOs, DOST of provinces/cities and NAFOSTED. Links with other work packages. The WP links with ‘Anthropogenic Pollution’ Core Theme (CT$_2$). [SOURCE: Page_60]

CT$_3$-WP10: Advances waste treatment [SOURCE: Page_60]

People involved: Xuan-Thanh Bui (HCMUT), David Riassetto (LMGP), Céline Ternon (LMGP), Florian Breider (EPFL), Ngo Huu Hao (UTS), Bui Manh Ha (SGU), Nguyen Cong Nguyen (DLU), Linh-Thy Le (UMP), Varong Pavarajarn (CU), Kowit Lertwittayanonh (POSU), Watsa Khongnakorn (POSU) Context and knowledge gaps: The sustainable eco-social development in Vietnam is facing important challenges in looking for effective wastewater pollution controls, feasible water reclamation or reuse methods, and eco-friendly solid waste disposals and recycling. Membrane-based technologies combining with biological processes or the advanced oxidation processes have widely applied for municipal wastewater reclamation (Jing et al, 2021). The use of anaerobic digestion or anaerobic membrane bioreactors for degradable organic solid waste or high organic strength wastewater can produce [SOURCE: Page_60]

59 [SOURCE: Page_60]

remarkable biogas as a renewable energy source that is efficiently replaced for fossil fuel and the digestate can be used as fertilizer for agriculture. Scientific questions and main objective: The scientific questions related the advanced waste treatment include: What are the advanced waste treatment technologies that may be effectively employed for wastewater reuse or waste recycling in Vietnam? What are integrated approaches that can bring a paradigm change in managing water or solid waste from “wastewater treatment or solid waste disposal” to “profitable utilization toward a circular economy? The main objective of the WP is to find suitable advanced waste treatment approaches toward a circular economy in Vietnam. Material and Methods: Methodology for this WP is illustrated by the following works: To overview on wastewater reuse applications and the novel waste treatment processes, To set-up and operating the lab-scale and pilot-scale experiments of the advanced waste treatment processes, To evaluate efficiency of the advanced waste treatment approaches to be tested in the experiments. Expected outcomes and current project funding initiatives: The expected outcomes of the WP are: (i) to determine potential applications of the advanced waste treatment approaches for wastewater reuse and waste recycling in Vietnam and (ii) to evaluate quantitatively environmental and cost benefits of wastewater reuse or waste recycling applications. The potential fundings may be found from research programs of VNU-HCM, IRD, NGOs, DOST of provinces/cities and NAFOSTED. Links with other work packages: The WP links with ‘Anthropogenic Pollution’ Core Theme (CT$_2$). [SOURCE: Page_61]

Core Theme 4 (CT$_4$): Hazards in Coastal zone Tran Hai Yen and Marchesiello Patrick [SOURCE: Page_61]

CT$_4$-WP11: Coastal erosion [SOURCE: Page_61]

People involved: Tran Hai Yen (HCMUT), Patrick Marchesiello (IRD/LEGOS), Duong Hai Thuan (TLU), Nguyen Nguyet Minh (SWIRR), Rafael Almar (IRD), Guillaume Morvan (IRD) Partners: Thuyloi University (TLU), Southern Institute of Water Resources Research (SIWRR) Context and knowledge gaps:The coastal areas are densely populated and play an important role in the Vietnamese economy. Therefore, their protection is an imperative necessity. The coastal Mekong delta, is now affected by human activity, including land subsidence, reduction of river fluxes due to damming and sand mining, and reduction of protective coastal mangroves in favor of agriculture and aquaculture. Our objectives emerged from a previous AFD/EU project (PI: P. Marchesiello) that revisited the paradigms needed for understanding and mediating coastal vulnerability and identified knowledge gaps (land subsidence, role of climate variability patterns). There is a clear need to improve physics-based models and deepen our understanding of coastal hydro-morphodynamics in both muddy and sandy environments, then apply this knowledge to improve empirical long-term shoreline evolution models (one-line models).] Scientific questions and main objective: The main scientific objectives and questions concern the hierarchy of processes affecting the state and evolution of the Mekong coastal delta and the sandy coasts of central Vietnam. In particular: what is the variability of ocean forcing affecting coastal erosion? How is the coastline changing and is this change related to climate variability and change? How can we improve physics-based models to correctly simulate the coastal sediment budget and its evolution, including with protection measures? Can we build reliable datasets from coastal cameras and satellite imagery to improve empirical models of long-term coastline evolution so that they provide good predictive capabilities at low cost? Material and Methods: The methods are multiple but give a large place to numerical modeling (CROCO, PLAXIS-3D, ShoreFor). In particular, this WP will benefit from the support of the GDR and GDRI-South CROCO for training and networking within the modeling community. For the assessment of climate impacts, global reanalyses and climate projections will be used. TLU researchers will conduct in situ measurements of nearshore waves and bathymetry to calibrate and validate models, and monitor shoreline changes and morphological dynamics using satellite imagery, cameras, and drones. SIWRR will also make its physical testing facilities (flumes and basins) available as funding permits. Expected outcomes and current project funding initiatives: Current projects funded (VNUHCM) in HCMUT concern the improvement of simplified one-line models and the building of high-resolution hydrodynamics and sediment transport maps in central Vietnam from 3D models (PI: Tran Hai Yen). Another current project in central Vietnam (PI: Nguyen Viet, TLU, largely involving Duong Hai Thuan) is to develop modeling systems to assess and predict morphological changes and countermeasures to stabilize beaches. Support for similar projects for the Mekong Delta will be sought. Links with other work packages: The relation to the WP on relative sea level is particularly important (CT$_4$-WP12). There is also a strong potential for interaction with the WP on tidal river hydrodynamics (CT$_1$-WP$_1$). [SOURCE: Page_61]

CT$_4$-WP12: Relative sea level rise [SOURCE: Page_61]

People involved: Ha Quang Khai (HCMUT), Jourdain Nicolas (CNRS-IGE, Grenoble), Dinh Ho-Tong-Minh (INRAE, Montpellier). Context and knowledge gaps: River deltas in Vietnam are threatened by significant land loss because of relative sea- level rise, i.e., a combination of land subsidence and absolute sea level rise. Land subsidence is largely related to the increasing 60 [SOURCE: Page_61]

groundwater extraction that accelerates the compaction of aquifer systems. Absolute sea level rise is related to climate change, with a mean contribution mostly related to thermal expansion of the ocean and to the melting Cryosphere. Changes in extreme events (typhoons and tides) may modulate the mean sea level rise. Both the subsidence and climatic components may significantly affect relative sea level rise over the 21st century, with relative importance depending on the local and global actions. Scientific questions and main objective: The key objectives related to land subsidence are to better understand the relationship between groundwater extraction and land subsidence for the Mekong Delta and Ho Chi Minh City and to investigate solutions for minimizing land subsidence. The key objectives related to absolute sea level rise are to estimate regional sea level rise in Vietnam related to climate change until 2150 for various global socio-economic scenarios, and to investigate potential changes in extreme sea level events. Material and Methods: Monitoring of land subsidence will be achieved through the use of Interferometric Synthetic Aperture Radar (InSAR) applied to Southern Vietnam. Groundwater modelling will be used to investigate remediation of land subsidence for current and potential water extraction strategies. Estimates of climate-induced sea level rise will be achieved through sea level modelling fed by updated projections of the Cryosphere contribution and through regional ocean and atmosphere modelling. Expected outcomes and current project funding initiatives: From this WP, we expect to get insight into the amplitudes and remediation strategies for current and future relative sea level rise in the Mekong Delta and Ho Chi Minh City region. The land subsidence part benefited from projects such as “Governance of Land Subsidence and Groundwater Management in the Mekong Delta Vietnam” that were funded by the Netherland Government in 2020-2021 and small funds from the GEMMES Viet Nam project. The climatic sea level part is partly funded by the PROTECT EU H2020 project. Links with other work packages: Links with WPs on coastal erosion (CT$_4$-WP11), high Impact precipitation and flooding (CT$_1$-WP$_3$). [SOURCE: Page_62]

Cross Cutting Theme (XCT): Vulnerabilities and resilience in the Saigon DongNai hydrological system and Mekong delta Ngo Thi Thu Trang, Hardy Sebastien [SOURCE: Page_62]

Until now, the results of scientific work already published on the Saigon DongNai system and on the Mekong delta from LECZ-CARE observed impacts of natural and societal hazards (flood, pollution, etc.) on the population. However, at the same time, these results stumble on solutions to resolve these impacts, mainly because these works do not fully integrate data on population, socio-urban dynamics and on governance. To improve the results in order to propose solutions, the Social sciences data could help to shed a different light on the results and implement an approach for also making it more operational, particularly to the Vietnamese stakeholders. For these reasons, the axis Vulnerabilities and resilience is a transversal axis intended to promote inter and transdisciplinary and proposes to develop the following WP: [SOURCE: Page_62]

XCT-WP13: Social database dedicated to Earth Sciences People involved: Sébastien Hardy (IGE-IRD), Pauline Gluski (PRODIG-IRD), Marlène Villanova-Oliver (LIG-UGA), Chon Le Trung (HCMUNRE) Context and knowledge gaps: In order to better meet the challenge of creating useful knowledge to solve problems and to help make decisions in the Saigon DongNai system and in the Mekong delta, the scientists need to pool their data and results to be able to propose solutions to some identified problems. However, the integration of data from different sciences, to derive new data, is a complex and interdisciplinary process. It requires in-depth work to build a database that pools different types of scientific results, including those of the social sciences, to draw new analyzes from them. Scientific questions and main objective: How to create useful data from different sciences at different spatial and temporal scales? Data such as hydrology data produced by the Surface Water and Ocean mission Topography (SWOT), spatial hydrology, TELEMAC and TONIC hydromodels about the infiltration capacity of the soils in a city for example, but also relevant social sciences data such as demographic, sociologic and economic information that will bring complementary contextual information. In this WP, a special emphasis will be put on the capacity of the database to address the spatial (georeferended) dimension but also the temporal one so to represent the evolution of indicators. For these reasons, the WP Social database dedicated to Earth Sciences propose to build a multidimensional model capable of integrating data produced by different scientific disciplines (geography, anthropology, hydrochemistry, hydrogeology, civil engineering, meteorology), and complex external data from organizations (drinking water and sewage companies, agricultural companies, statistics offices, etc.). For example, how to consider the large quantity of floating population (migrants) not registered in the censuses, thus better understand hydrochemistry issues? Material and Methods: The material will be provided by the other axes and the reflection on the exploitation of this data will be carried out within the WP, mobilizing different models of database creation. Interdisciplinary workshops will be organized beforehand with the WP teams to identify the data whose crossing will produce added value in the search for solutions. [SOURCE: Page_62]

61 [SOURCE: Page_62]

Expected outcomes and current project funding initiatives: The outcomes will be the implementation of a model of complex database using adapted technologies to exploit a database common to different sciences oriented towards problem solving. [SOURCE: Page_63]

XCT-WP14: Population practices and expectations, economic development strategies and public policies [SOURCE: Page_63]

People involved: Eve Bureau-Point (EHESS-CNRS), Jean-Philippe Venot (G-EAU, IRD), Sylvie Fanchette (CESSMA-IRD), Ngo Thi Thu Trang (HCMUSSH), HCMC Statistic Office, Lê Khánh Hưng (HCMUSSH) Context and knowledge gaps: Highlighting physical and bio-chemical processes is not enough to solve problems. Their resolution also implies knowing the relation of the populations with their environment: both their imaginaries and practices It also involves considering economic development strategies and public policies in the Saigon DongNai system and in the Mekong delta. Problems have to be understood regionally. Pollutants from Cambodia may flow to the South of Vietnam. So there is a need to study the environmental problems such as the chemical substances used in agricultural and industrial activities (very often located in the provinces around Ho Chi Minh) in the whole area. There is also a need of understanding why some products are used instead of less harmful techniques/ones. This information can be compared with chemical analysis of the residues found in the samples taken. The region is also a factory of the today’s globalized world whose industrial activities cause pollution and whose effects are massive but different according to the populations considered (in the first place, the factory workers, often made invisible, located outside the cities in precarious conditions, by complex mechanisms). Scientific questions and main objective: Are the physical and bio-chemical processes highlighted by the earth sciences considered as problems by the populations? Are its populations affected directly or indirectly by these processes? If yes, how and why? How do private economic activities contribute to pollution? Do public policies attempt to respond to pollution problems and if so, how? The objective of the WP is to provide a better understanding of the environmental and health hazards experienced by the population. Material and Methods: Data collection will consist in collecting documents and conducting multi-sites surveys: semi- directed interviews with previously identified stakeholders, observations, comparative surveys on practices (pesticide, antibiotics, use of the well water, etc.). It will also involve analyzing the public policies implemented in order to understand their impact on the exposure of the population. Expected outcomes and current project funding initiatives: The result will be social data oriented to understand the environmental practices of stakeholders. The project REX AQUA (Retour d’Expériences, Apprentissages et diffusion d’information sur la gestion de l’eau (Feedback from experiences, learning and dissemination of information on water management), Centre des Politiques de la Terre (Sciences Po Paris), is under evaluation. [SOURCE: Page_63]

XCT-WP15: Risk assessments People involved: Sébastien Hardy (IGE-IRD), Ngo Thi Thu Trang (HCMUSSH), Pauline Gluski (PRODIG-IRD), Minh Thông (SAWACO), Lê Khánh Hưng (HCMUSSH), Isabelle Ruin (IGE-CNRS), Nguyen Thi My Lan (HCMUSSH) Context and knowledge gaps: To mobilize in an uncertain context data from different disciplines to derive to their better interpretations, the building of maps that show all what is at stake is an ideal mediation tool. Maps can help to make decisions of risk assessments by visualizing complex data on a territory. Scientific questions and main objective: This WP seeks to identify the vulnerability of territories in order to assess the impacts of natural and societal hazards on the territory and on the populations of the lower Mekong region. This gives decision-makers the possibility of identifying the territories in which to act as a priority by concentrating their means there, by proposing solutions of resilience, with chances of better effects. Material and Methods: In this WP, the GIS will be mobilized to build maps and multi-agent analyses. It is about looking at the spatio-temporal interrelationships of Ho Chi Minh with the provinces of the Southwest region of Vietnam. Transdisciplinary workshops will be organized before the publication of results. Expected outcomes and current project funding initiatives: Vulnerabilities maps, a metadata and a catalog of data for the restitution and the sharing, implementing the FAIR principle (Findable, Accessible, Interoperable, Reusable). The development of a serious game could be considered to help decision-makers to better coordinate around common risks to be resolved. The objective is to initiate an integrated risk assessment built on both interdisciplinary scientific results and stakeholders. The project Analyses of the vulnerability of the inhabitants to polluted water, District 12, Go Vap and Hoc Mon demonstrator, HCMC Department of science and Technology, is under evaluation. [SOURCE: Page_63]

3.3. Synergy with funded research projects Several large projects have just been granted and will significantly contribute to start the new IJL with a great research dynamic. Several projects are under review and other will be submitted in 2024. The different research projects funded are presented below, the research projects under review are presented in annex. Fig 2 is showing the local or regional studied areas under different ongoing research projects and fig. 3 shows the timeline of the projects within the IJL framework. [SOURCE: Page_63]

62 [SOURCE: Page_63]

Figure 2: Local and Regional studied areas via on-going research projects [SOURCE: Page_64]

PLUME Project (2024): The project, led by researchers from LEGOS, MIO and LOTUS, aims to study the transport of natural and anthropogenic dissolved and particulate matter in river plumes in Vietnam. The oceanic cruise will occur in May- June 2024. HCMUT and IGE researchers will be on board of the Anthea boat and will focus on the assessment of microplastics, emerging contaminants, nutrients, greenhouses gas fluxes along the continuum land-ocean in Mekong Delta and Saigon- Dong Nai systems. All researchers from Axis 2 are involved in this project. The TROPECOS project (2023-2028, PEPR FairCarbon), 1,4 M EUR: will structure the scientific community working on the coastal C cycle in three French ultramarine territories and two international research structures in Vietnam (Saigon DongNai estuary, Can Gio Mangrove and Mekong Delta) and Brazil. Thanks to original modelling approaches and remote sensing observations, the project be able to precisely compute air-sea CO$_2$, CH$_4$ and N2O fluxes from tropical deltas, tidal estuaries, lagoons and river plumes offshore, to analyse their evolution during the last 20 years and to predict future emissions under different coastal management scenarios. In mangroves, which are ecosystems with outstanding carbon storage capacity, we will deploy unprecedented collective sampling efforts, thanks to the expertise and geographical distribution of the TROPECOS consortium. SAWACO Project (2024-2026), 200 k EUR: The two governmental agencies SAWACO (Saigon Water Coorporation who produce drinking water for the entire population of HCMC) and DOST (Department of Science and Technology) are founded this project. We will monitor the occurrence of emerging contaminants in surface water from drinking water catchment areas. We will then monitor the emerging contaminants and disinfection-by-products generated along the drinking water treatment chain and in the final drinking water products. Finally, based on our dataset and on surveys on people practices we will perform a human risk assessment. The researchers from A$_2$/WP$_2$ (emerging contaminants, Axis^3/WP$_1$ on drinking water management and Axis^5 on social sciences are all involved in this project) MULTISOURCES Project (2021-2025), 5 M EUR: The overall goal of this project is to, together with local, national, and international stakeholders, demonstrate a variety of about the Enhanced Natural Treatment Solutions (ENTS) treating a wide range of urban waters and to develop innovative tools, methods, and business models that support city planning and long-term operations and maintenance of natural based solutions (NbS) for water treatment, storage, and reuse in urban areas worldwide. HCMUT, one of international stakeholders, is being mapped and analysed from two different perspectives, from the perspective of the ENTS Pilots and from the perspective of the relevant Work Packages responsible for developing the Business Models, a web-based decision-support tool for stakeholders to facilitate the selection and NbS design and the Planning Platform. OBS-Viet Project (2021-2024) 63 k EUR: This grant, funded by IGE laboratory, is a financial booster for the implementation of new research topics such as Black Carbon and air quality, precipitation at high impact and social sciences. MarMiB Project (2022 - 2028) 8.5 k EUR: This project is funded by GENØK - SENTER FOR BIOSIKKERHET, Norway with the participation of researchers from Norway, Germany, South Africa, Costa Rica and Vietnam. The project aims to assess the linkage between biofilms forming on microplastics (MP), spread of antimicrobial resistance genes (AMRGs) in biofilms and marine ecology. During 2023 - 2024 period, two sampling campaigns will be conducted in Can Thanh beach of the Can Gio mangrove, downstream of Ho Chi Minh City. [SOURCE: Page_64]

63 [SOURCE: Page_64]

Arsenic Project (2023-2025) This project is funded by NAFOSTED. The aim of the project is to study the impact of hydrological conditions on the fate of arsenic in the aquifer system and to understand the C, N and S cycle impact on the Arsenic mobilization. An intensive sampling campaign is on-going for an 18-month period to monitor: groundwater level, temperature, also collect groundwater samples every month for analysis of geochemical characteristics, Trace metal, As and stable isotope. Water samples are also sampled for microbiological analysis (protein, DNA): This project is in strong collaboration with EPFL (Prof Rizlan, co PI of the project). In 2023, some hydrogeophysical tools will be used to study the site in order to facilitate the building of an hydrological model for futures simulations [SOURCE: Page_65]

3.4 Education, Training and transfer activities The research plan will involve the training of students from Master’s to PhD degree. The students will be involved in research projects, learn the different skills and tools in their relevant research fields, participate in scientific meetings at the national and international level. For the coming years, 4 PhD students (Nguyen Van Tu, Amaral F., Thanh Truong Quoc, Ngoc Dinh Tuy Vi), 2 postdocs (Nguyen Truong An and one currently being recruited) and one research engineer (Nguyen Thi Ngoc Tuyet) will be involved in the research and we aim to apply for funds to recruit 4 additional PhD students (through ARTS, French embassy calls as well as through submitted research projects: ANR). CARE will keep a strong connection with the education framework implemented during LECZ-CARE (MOOC “Des Rivières et des Hommes” available in Vietnamese, PFIEV launched in 2020 at HCMUT that keeps increasing its enrolment rate, Eturescif which facilitate and promote student visits from foreign engineering schools within the international RESCIF network). IRN network connected with CARE will contribute to the knowledge transfer through training school IRN-Sud CROCO and through continuing training of HCMUT staff with recurrent long term visits (MLD) of Marchesiello P. and Morvan G., IRN Passeport-2C). From 2025, a summer school will be organized every year on “Water Quality and Emerging Threats” and open to all masters and PhD students of the south region of Vietnam (Can Tho university, Ben Tre university…) and to Cambodians partner’s students from ITC (cf summer school plan in annex). Moreover, through IJL-2 we will organize scientific seminars every two months and invite Vietnamese and international researchers to give a talk to pursue the scientific exchange and maintain a good research dynamic. Such seminars will gather all early career scientists to broaden their multidisciplinary knowledge and to develop their international networks. [SOURCE: Page_65]

3.5 Objectives for resource, capacity building and development Our first objective will be to strengthen CARE chemical laboratory facilities to pursue our goal to establish CARE building as a leading scientific hub by implementing new scientific and sampling equipment needed for the different research topics involved in CARE. Since the creation of CARE lab, several analytical instruments as well as water quality monitoring field equipment have been acquired and made available to Vietnamese and international researchers. We aim to pursue this dynamic by acquiring new equipment such as DOC analyzer, LC and GC-MS, equipment in the framework of SAWACO, PLUME and TROPECOS projects. Through our research actions we will continue to train and employ technicians to establish a long term monitoring during CARE (hydrodynamics, eutrophication, emerging contaminants concentrations in drinking water and surface water in link with SAWACO and DOST, arsenic contamination in Mekong Delta, Black carbon in surface water and air). Such monitoring will be implemented in collaboration with stakeholders. We will establish a specimen bank of environmental samples (sediment, passive samplers, wastewaters etc…) for future Vietnamese researchers. The samples will be archived in freezers for long term storage. In the future, they will allow retrospective studies as new research questions emerge. They will also allow to study time trends and evaluate the impact of new environmental management practices (implementation of wastewater treatment plants, regulations, implantation of new industrial parks etc …) Our third objective is to establish a reliable and freely accessible socio-environmental observation system. This observation system will be built by integrating all data produced through the different research projects and our long-term monitoring. We will draft a data management plan which will include: the data needs over the project, data descriptions and how and when the data will be collected, stored and accessed. All data will be archived in Datasuds, the publicly accessible international data center supported by IRD. Particular attention will be paid to following the Guidelines on FAIR Data Management to make our research Data Findable, Accessible, Interoperable and Reusable Our fourth objective will be to ensure efficient dissemination activities. Dissemination activities will be linked to the exploitation of results within and outside the CARE IJL community. As the majority of partners work in scientific research and education institutes, our main dissemination objectives are to (i) advance knowledge through high impact scientific publications, (ii) inform policy makers and stakeholders about risk assessment and hazard mitigation, (iii) provide expertise and project results to associated stakeholders, (iv) support education of the next generation by using CARE results in teaching during our summer school and (v) inform the general population. We will use the results from CARE to strengthen existing partnerships and develop new ones, while raising the profile of CARE researchers through publications. Indeed, promoting the results of the projects will advance their careers and strengthen their ability to attract further research funding at local and international scale. Importantly, we will then establish a communication unit to improve the transfer of research findings toward research institutions, stakeholders and importantly toward the Vietnamese population. The communication unit will also support any initiatives aimed at promoting CARE as a sub-regional center of excellence and at increasing the visibility of [SOURCE: Page_65]

64 [SOURCE: Page_65]

its research projects and scientific outcomes at the national and international level. The dedicated website will be updated during all the IJL life and will be one way to increase our visibility and to communicate with general populations. Through the IJL we will do our best to promote gender equality in research and innovation, by ensuring a leading role to women researchers (co-direction of themes and work packages), we will support young female PhD and master students and encourage them to pursue their career at the academic level. We will constrain all important annual meetings (CARE’s Scientific Days, RESCIF meeting, bi-annual conference that we support WSTG “Water Sustainable and Green Technology” and the IRD regional workshops sharing with Hanoi, Pnomh Penh, Bangkok) within a same period of time each year to limit travel and ensure a low carbon economy. Other meetings and scientific seminars will be organized online. [SOURCE: Page_66]

3.6 IJL-2 Timeline of IJL: [SOURCE: Page_66]

The timing of the different research projects, scientific animations, training activities and structural activities as well as expatriation plan is presented in the Gantt chart in figure 3. Plan for MLD (long-term stay) are presented in annexe. It should be highlight that several expatriations are foreseen in 2024-2025: Since the previous expatriation peak (2015-2018), only one or two people have been expatriated (Emilie Strady (until sept 2021) and Marc Descloitres, alone since 2021). It is time to “reboost”                                                   CARE’s                                                    dynamism. [SOURCE: Page_66]

Figure 3: Timeline of IJL [SOURCE: Page_66]

4. Scientific project versus development stakes: balance between advancement of knowledge and impact on development issues. [SOURCE: Page_66]

Our research project will contribute to a better understanding of the environment, its functioning, degradation and evolution prediction over time through different scenarios (climate change, different water management strategies, implementation of nature based solutions, implementations of wastewater treatment plants etc…). CARE will help to train the next generation of Vietnamese researchers (through Master and PhD supervision, summer school, joined publications) and set up operational structure (implementation of long term monitoring, open database, building a specimen bank, buying equipment to CARE laboratory) to ensure that it last beyond IJL project. The exploitation activities will integrate new knowledge with the aim of transforming the IJL-2 results into tangible outcomes that can be applied to practical decision-making processes. These activities will include both academic exploitation and direct use by a range of stakeholders and companies. They include city/provincial Authorities such as Departments of Natural Resources and Environment, Departments of Construction, Environmental Monitoring Centers, water and wastewater companies such as SAWACO, HCMC Urban Drainage Company URENCO, local environmental quality analysis/treatment technology consultancy enterprises like ETM, etc. Concerning anthropogenic contaminations CARE results will inform stakeholders and utilities about the sources and level of risk associated with different water systems, guide priorities and potential actions for risk management (setting up guidelines, regulation on pesticides, increase communication on the danger linked to antibiotics use, plastic ban etc….). Based on our previous work and future large scale sampling campaigns we will be able to select relevant sites for future long-term monitoring plan. Concerning hazard in coastal zone, scenarios taking into account regional land and ground-water use as well as global socio-economic pathways will be used at time scales that matter for decision makers. CARE is moving towards a more holistic and cross-disciplinary approach to hydrological system research, where hydrological systems are considered as social- environmental systems. This approach will enable us to generate an assessment of threats, resilience, and adaptation across the delta and HCMC region. We will use maps that show all the issues and threats as an ideal mediation tool. We will identify the vulnerability of territories in order to assess the impacts of natural and societal hazards on the territory and on the 65 [SOURCE: Page_66]

populations of the lower Mekong region. This will give decision-makers the possibility of identifying the territories in which to act as a priority by concentrating their means there, by proposing solutions of resilience, with chances of better effects. Based on the outcomes, protection strategies will be proposed to stakeholders. The development of a simulation game could be considered to help decision-makers to better coordinate their action around a specific crisis to be resolved. Thanks to our communication unit, we will raise Vietnamese population awareness about environmental issues. Vietnam is among the countries with the highest number of social media users worldwide. We will therefore use social media, especially Facebook (Facebook is the main social media used by the Vietnamese population), to disseminate in Vietnamese and at high frequency the obtained knowledge and results. [SOURCE: Page_67]

### 4. Description of partnership and governance.

4.1 Partner laboratories and institutes Due to the wide range of expertise needed to tackle the different challenges occurring in the region, our research projects need the involvement of scientists from various disciplines. Since 2015, CARE has a close partnership with four faculties HCMUT FERN (2 pers), HCMUT FCE (4 pers), HCMUT-GeoPet (4 pers), HCMUT FChE (3 pers) (detailed description can be found in annex Table A$_1$). In addition to the faculties, a close partnership has been built since 2018 with the VNU-HCM KEY lab. New partnerships have been established as a result of the expansion of the IJL scientific scope with different faculties such as: HCMUSSH (2 pers): The University of Social Sciences and Humanities, Vietnam National University of HCMC was established in 1957.The university is one of the two biggest institutions in the field of social sciences and humanities in Vietnam. The university is the pioneering institution in offering new academic programs to meet societal demands such as Vietnamese Studies, Oriental Studies, Anthropology, International Relations, Urban Studies and so on. HCMUNRE (2 pers): The HCM University of Natural Resources and Environment of Ho Chi Minh City is the only public university in the South of Vietnam under the Ministry of Natural Resources and Environment. HCMUNRE has affirmed its position in the higher education system in the fields of resource management, ensuring a clean environment for sustainable development and deal with global climate change issues. HCMUS (4 pers): Formerly known as University of Sciences, the university provides several educations on Biology, Chemistry, Environment, Geology, Information Technology, Math & Computer Science, Materials Science, Oceanography and Physics. Collaborations in south Vietnam will be developed with ITC in Cambodia, Can Tho and Ben Tre university in south of Vietnam. On the French side, scientists from LECZ-CARE and new scientists are getting on board from IGE (24 pers), G-EAU (5 pers), LEGOS (4 pers), MIO (1 pers), G-INP (4 pers). Indeed, the success of IJL LECZ-CARE and its research dynamic brought new scientists to join IJL CARE, phase 2. A new institution, INRAE, with several researchers (5 pers) from Riverly, REversaal and TETIS are joining CARE on various topics such as emerging contaminants, nature-based solutions to remediate wastewater, and subsidence in the Mekong Delta. As mentioned previously, a major innovation in CARE is to incorporate social sciences into the different research topics to better deal with socio-environmental issues. So, Geographers from PRODIG (1 pers), CESSMA (1 pers), G-EAU (1 pers) and anthropologist from Centre Norbert Elisa (1 pers) are on board. The IJL CARE-P$_2$ is also strong with its international connections through its research projects and collaborations: Canadian with Trent University and Polytechnic Montreal, EPFL in Switzerland, SLU in Sweden which aims to promote CARE laboratory as an international scientific hub. [SOURCE: Page_67]

4.2 Governance [SOURCE: Page_67]

The organizational structure has been designed to have (i) efficient interactions and communication between all partners and associated stakeholders, (ii) synergies with other international initiatives and networks related to water pollution and hazards in coastal zone. A clear distribution of responsibilities and a sound flow of information are planned along three main levels (Fig. 4). [SOURCE: Page_67]

66 [SOURCE: Page_67]

Figure 4: CARE’s Governance and structural organization (2024-2029). [SOURCE: Page_68]

Decision board: the two IJL co-directors, will manage the financial and human resources from IJL funds (IRD, Grenoble INP, CNRS and possibly INRAE and related support from French labs) and coordinate research activities. They are responsible for the smooth running of the work (partnerships, field missions, etc.). They will be helped by with IJL deputy- director Frédéric Cazenave, who will be based in HCMC from January 2024, The administrative CARE lab director (named by HCMUT) and the administrative deputy director will manage the financial and human resources from VNU-HCMUT funds. This decision board will meet as a “Com-Dir” each week at CARE lab for discussion on weekly organization, budget, research projects, research visits etc. They organize strategic meetings between current and potential partners and act as a link between the Steering Committee and all IJL members. They are responsible for disseminating information at all levels of the internal structure of IJL and toward the external community via the communication unit (internet, public, decision-makers, etc.). Executive board: All leaders, scientists and students involved in the different work packages are responsible for ensuring that the tasks are carried out properly and in agreement with the rules of ethics and scientific integrity. The leaders of scientific themes will communicate with the different work package leaders involved once every three months. The aims of the scientific theme leaders are to ensure the dissemination of the information with the other work packages leaders, to help to pool resources (human resources, organize common sampling campaigns etc...). All French theme co-leaders will be expatriate researchers to contribute to the local and regional reinforcement of the partnership and ensure a good communication with the local partners. Steering Committee: The steering committee consists of the executive board, the representative of the HCMUT faculties involved in CARE (Deans and/or vice deans), the executive board of French labs (UMRs). The role of the steering committee will be to ensure that the IJL strategy is on a good track. Projects progress will be presented, the financial and human resources used will be displayed, risk management and difficulties encountered will be discussed. The steering committee is invited at the “scientific days” External scientific advisory board: This board will include recognized international scientists working on south East- Asia issues such as Renaud Fabrice (Glasgow university) and LMI LOTUS directors Hermann M. and Ngo Duc Thanh. The members will be invited every year at our scientific day meetings and they will provide a critical and constructive look at the different actions carried out. For optimal internal interactions within the consortium, CARE will organize a yearly meeting called “CARE’s scientific days” dedicated to the restitution of all scientific outcomes and an update on all ongoing projects. IJL directors and work package leaders will lead the meetings. Stakeholders, IRN network leaders, French Consulate, external advisory board, Deans of HCMUT and from all partners (HCMUS, HCMUNRE, HCMSH), executive secretary of RESCIF members as well as potential partners will be invited to the meeting and encouraged to share ideas and to initiate collaboration. [SOURCE: Page_68]
